.
Additional Feynman diagrams in 1D AKSZ with general polarization on
X^{[0]}_{\mathrm{out}}
.

In total, we obtain the effective action (
53
).
∎

Remark 5.6
.

In the main case of interest for this paper, the target
\mathfrak{g}=\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
is infinite-dimensional and the propagator contains a delta form as the “inverse” of the pairing
(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=\int_{M}\tau_{1}\wedge\tau_{2}
(cf. Remark
4.1
).
However, our computations here are still valid. Indeed, even though Feynman diagrams contain products of delta functions, since all these diagrams are actually trees, no problematic terms like
\delta(0)
arise when computing the integrals.

Proposition 5.7
.

The lagrangian generated by (
53
)
is the evolution relation in
X_{\mathrm{in}}^{[0]}\times X_{\mathrm{out}}^{[0]}
.

Proof.

We know that in the
\psi^{\pm}
variables, the evolution relation is given by
\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{out}}=\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{in}}+d^{-}_{\mathfrak{g}}A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}}
,
\psi^{+}_{\mathrm{out}}=\psi^{+}_{\mathrm{in}}+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}}
. The lagrangian generated by
S_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{ph}}[\psi_{\mathrm{in}}^{-},\psi_{\mathrm{out}}^{Q},A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}}]
is

\displaystyle-\psi^{+}_{\mathrm{in}}=\frac{\partial S_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{ph}}}{\partial\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{in}}}

\displaystyle=-F(\psi_{\mathrm{in}}^{-}+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}},\psi^{Q}_{\mathrm{out}})+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}}

\displaystyle=-F(\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{out}},\psi^{Q}_{\mathrm{out}})+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}}=-\psi^{+}_{\mathrm{out}}+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}},

\displaystyle\psi^{P}_{\mathrm{out}}=\frac{\partial S_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{ph}}}{\partial\psi^{Q}_{\mathrm{out}}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial G(\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{out}},\psi_{\mathrm{out}}^{Q})}{\partial\psi^{Q}_{\mathrm{out}}},

\displaystyle 0=\frac{\partial S_{\mathrm{ph}}}{\partial A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}}}

\displaystyle=d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}\psi_{\mathrm{in}}^{-}+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}\psi^{+}_{\mathrm{out}}+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}}.

This lagrangian coincides with the evolution relation.
∎

5.2.3.
Modified quantum master equation

Let us also comment on the mQME. Again, we can compute the BV bracket (we ignore higher ghosts for simplicity)

\frac{1}{2}\{S_{\mathrm{eff}},S_{\mathrm{eff}}\}_{\mathrm{res}}=\{S_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{gh}},S_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{ph}}\}=-(\psi^{[1]}_{\mathrm{out}}-\psi^{[1]}_{\mathrm{in}},d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}F(\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{in}}+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}},\psi_{\mathrm{out}}^{Q})+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{in}}+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}}).

As before, we have
\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}=-(d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}\psi_{\mathrm{in}}^{[1]},\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{in}})-i\hbar(d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}\psi_{\mathrm{in}}^{[1]},\frac{\delta}{\delta\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{in}}})
and

\displaystyle Z^{-1}\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}Z

\displaystyle=-(d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}\psi_{\mathrm{in}}^{[1]},\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{in}})Z-(d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}\psi^{[1]}_{\mathrm{in}},F(\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{in}}+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}},\psi_{\mathrm{out}}^{Q}))+(d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}\psi^{[1]}_{\mathrm{in}},d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}})

\displaystyle=-(\psi^{[1]}_{\mathrm{in}},d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}F(\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{in}}+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}},\psi_{\mathrm{out}}^{Q})+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{in}}+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}}).

Thus, the mQME is equivalent to

(54)

Z^{-1}\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}Z=(\psi^{[1]}_{\mathrm{out}},d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}F(\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{in}}+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}},\psi_{\mathrm{out}}^{Q})+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{in}}+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}})=(\psi^{[1]}_{\mathrm{out}},d_{\mathfrak{g}}\psi^{[0]}_{\mathrm{out}}).

The operator
\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}
acting on ghost number 0 fields should be obtained as a quantization of
\Theta(\psi)
in the
\psi^{P}_{\mathrm{out}},\psi^{Q}_{\mathrm{out}}
variables,

(55)

\Theta(\psi^{P}_{\mathrm{out}},\psi^{Q}_{\mathrm{out}})=(d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}\psi^{[1]}_{\mathrm{out}},\psi^{-}(\psi^{P}_{\mathrm{out}},\psi^{Q}_{\mathrm{out}}))+(d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}\psi^{[1]}_{\mathrm{out}},\psi^{+}(\psi^{P}_{\mathrm{out}},\psi^{Q}_{\mathrm{out}})).

The standard quantization
\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathrm{std}}
of (
55
) — i.e., replacing all
\psi^{P}_{\mathrm{out}}
variables with
-i\hbar\delta/\delta\psi^{Q}_{\mathrm{out}}
and moving all derivatives to the right - satisfies (
54
) to
0
-th order in
\hbar
, but there are terms of higher order in
\hbar
corresponding to higher derivatives in
\psi^{Q}_{\mathrm{out}}
acting on
G
. To prove the mQME to all orders, one would have to find quantum corrections to
\Omega^{\mathrm{std}}_{\mathrm{out}}
such that these terms are cancelled and the deformed operator still squares to 0.

Remark 5.8
.

A particularly simple case occurs when
\psi(\psi^{P},\psi^{Q})=\psi^{P}+\psi^{Q}
. A rather trivial example of this case is
\psi^{Q}=\psi^{+},\psi^{P}=\psi^{-}
. A nontrivial example will be considered in Section
6.3
. In this case, we may define

\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}=\left(d_{\mathfrak{g}}\psi^{[1]}_{\mathrm{out}},\psi^{Q}_{\mathrm{out}}-i\hbar\frac{\delta}{\delta\psi^{Q}_{\mathrm{out}}}\right).

Then, from
\partial G/\partial\psi^{Q}=\psi^{P}
we immediately get
Z^{-1}\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}Z=(d_{\mathfrak{g}}\psi^{[1]}_{\mathrm{out}},\psi^{Q}_{\mathrm{out}}+\psi^{P}_{\mathrm{out}})=(\psi^{[1]}_{\mathrm{out}},d_{\mathfrak{g}}\psi^{[0]}_{\mathrm{out}})
, i.e., Equation (
54
), and hence the mQME, are satisfied.
In general, we have the mQME whenever the constraints are linear both in the original and the new momenta, see
[
16
, Section 12]
.

Remark 5.9
.

Let
Z_{\mathrm{nl},\perp}
be the partition function with transversal ghost polarization and a general polarization in ghost number 0, to be precise, we are choosing the
(\psi^{-},\psi^{[<0]})
on the
\mathrm{in}
-boundary and the
(\psi^{Q},\psi^{[>0]})
on the
\mathrm{out}
-boundary.
In this case, there are no residual fields, and following a computation similar to the above, one finds
38
38

38

There are no bulk vertices in this polarization. The two contributing terms come from multivalent
\mathrm{out}
-boundary vertices in ghost number 0 and univalent boundary vertices in the ghost sector.

Z_{\mathrm{nl},\perp}=\exp\left(-\frac{i}{\hbar}G(\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{in}},\psi^{Q}_{\mathrm{out}})+\frac{i}{\hbar}\psi^{[<0]}_{\mathrm{in}}\psi^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{out}}\right).

The mQME for this partition function is just
(\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}+\Omega_{\mathrm{out}})Z=0
, since there are no residual fields. We can observe that the only obstruction for the mQME to hold is the existence of a suitable
\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}
in a general polarization.
Then, one can obtain the partition function
Z_{\mathrm{nl},\parallel}
-with parallel ghost polarization and
(\psi^{-},\psi^{Q})
-representation in ghost number 0, as given by (
53
) by composition of the partition function
Z_{\mathrm{l},\parallel}
with parallel ghost polarization and linear polarization in ghost number 0, given by (
46
) with the partition function
Z_{\mathrm{nl},\perp}
:
Z_{\mathrm{nl},\parallel}=Z_{\mathrm{nl},\perp}\circ Z_{\mathrm{l},\parallel}
. Since we know that
Z_{\mathrm{l},\parallel}
satisfies the mQME,
Z_{\mathrm{nl},\parallel}
will satisfy it if
Z_{\mathrm{nl},\perp}
does.

5.3.
3D nonabelian Chern–Simons with parallel ghost polarization and antiholomorphic-to-holomorphic polarization in ghost degree zero

Next, we return to the example of 3D Chern–Simons with parallel ghost polarization. In this context, it is convenient to use the traditional notation for the components of the superfield
\mathcal{A}
:

\mathcal{A}=c+A+A^{*}+c^{*},

where
\phi^{*}
denotes the BV antifield of the field
\phi
.

In this section we will use some special notations for field components (as compared to Section
4
):
{a^{1,0}=\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{fl}}^{1,0}}
,
a^{0,1}=\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{fl}}^{0,1}
,
c=\mathsf{A}^{0}
,
\mathsf{A}^{*}=\mathsf{A}^{2}
,
\sigma=\mathsf{A}^{0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}
.

5.3.1.
Abelian case

The action with polarization terms is:

S^{f}=\int_{I\times\Sigma}\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}d\mathcal{A}+\int_{\{1\}\times\Sigma}\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{A}^{1,0}\mathsf{A}^{0,1}+c\mathsf{A}^{*}\right)-\int_{\{0\}\times\Sigma}\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{A}^{0,1}\mathsf{A}^{1,0}+c\mathsf{A}^{*}\right).

The space of fields is:

\mathcal{F}=\Omega^{\bullet}(I,\Omega^{1,0}\oplus\Omega^{0,1}\oplus\Omega^{0}[1]\oplus\Omega^{2}[-1])

— here
\Omega^{p}
in the coefficients stands for
\Omega^{p}(\Sigma)
.
It is fibered over

\mathcal{B}=(\Omega^{0,1}\oplus\Omega^{0}[1])\bigoplus(\Omega^{1,0}\oplus\Omega^{0}[-1])\;\;\ni((\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},c_{\mathrm{in}}),\;(\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},c_{\mathrm{out}}))

with fiber

\mathcal{Y}=\Omega^{\bullet}(I,\{0\};\Omega^{0,1})\oplus\Omega^{\bullet}(I,\{1\};\Omega^{1,0})\oplus\Omega^{\bullet}(I,\{0,1\};\Omega^{0}[1])\oplus\Omega^{\bullet}(I;\Omega^{2}[-1]).

The space of residual fields is given by the (relative) cohomology in
I
-direction:

\mathcal{V}=H^{\bullet}(I,\{0,1\};\Omega^{0}[1])\oplus H^{\bullet}(I;\Omega^{2}[-1])\quad\ni(dt\cdot\sigma,\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}}).

The gauge-fixing lagrangian
\mathcal{L}
in the fiber of
\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
is given by setting to zero the (relatively) exact 1-form components of fields along
I
.

Thus, on
\mathcal{L}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{gh}=0\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\quad

\displaystyle A^{(1)}=\widetilde{\mathsf{A}}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}+\widetilde{\mathsf{A}}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}+a^{1,0}+a^{0,1}+dt\cdot\sigma,

\displaystyle\mathrm{gh}=1\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\quad

\displaystyle A^{(0)}=\widetilde{c}_{\mathrm{out}}+\widetilde{c}_{\mathrm{in}}+c_{\mathrm{fl}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{gh}=-1\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\quad

\displaystyle A^{(2)}=\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}}+\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{fl}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{gh}=-2\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\quad

\displaystyle A^{(3)}=0,

with tilde denoting the discontinuous extension by zero from
t=1
or
t=0
, respectively. Fluctuations are understood to satisfy

a^{1,0}|_{t=1}=0,\quad a^{0,1}|_{t=0}=0,\quad c_{\mathrm{fl}}|_{t=0}=c_{\mathrm{fl}}|_{t=1}=0,\quad\int_{0}^{1}dt\;\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{fl}}=0.

The gauge-fixed polarized action is:

S^{f}|_{\mathcal{L}}=\\
\int_{I\times\Sigma}a^{1,0}d_{I}a^{0,1}+\int_{I\times\Sigma}dt\,(a^{1,0}+a^{0,1})d_{\Sigma}\sigma+\int_{\Sigma}\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}a^{0,1}\big|_{t=1}-\int_{\Sigma}\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}a^{1,0}\big|_{t=0}\\
+\int_{I\times\Sigma}\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{fl}}d_{I}c_{\mathrm{fl}}-\int_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}}+\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{fl}}\big|_{t=1})c_{\mathrm{out}}+\int_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}}+\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{fl}}\big|_{t=0})c_{\mathrm{in}}.

The propagators are given by:

(56)

\displaystyle\langle a^{0,1}(t,z)a^{1,0}(t^{\prime},z^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=-i\hbar\,\theta(t-t^{\prime})\,\delta^{(2)}(z-z^{\prime})\frac{i}{2}d\bar{z}\,dz^{\prime},

(57)

\displaystyle\langle c_{\mathrm{fl}}(t,z)\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{fl}}(t^{\prime},z^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=-i\hbar\,(\theta(t-t^{\prime})-t)\,\delta^{(2)}(z-z^{\prime})\frac{i}{2}dz^{\prime}\,d\bar{z}^{\prime}.

The corresponding effective action is:

(58)

S^{\mathrm{eff}}=\int_{\Sigma}\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}+\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}\bar{\partial}\sigma+\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}\partial\sigma-\frac{1}{2}\partial\sigma\bar{\partial}\sigma-\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}}(c_{\mathrm{out}}-c_{\mathrm{in}}).

Remark 5.10

(Hamilton–Jacobi property, mQME)
.

Notice that (
58
) coincides with (
46
) above upon specializing
\mathfrak{g}=\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma)
,
X^{+}=\Omega^{1,0}(\Sigma)
,
X^{-}=\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma)
. Thus (
58
) satisfies the modified quantum master equation, and the
\mathrm{gh}=0
part of (
58
) generates the evolution relation of abelian Chern–Simons theory.

Remark 5.11

(Integrating out residual fields)
.

As in Section
4.2.2
, we can integrate out the residual fields
\sigma,\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{*}
by choosing a Riemannian metric compatible with the complex structure and decomposing fields as
\sigma=\sigma_{c}+\underline{\sigma},\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}}=\mu\cdot\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res},c}+\underline{\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}}}
. As expected, the result differs from (
34
) only in the ghost sector:

Z_{*}[c_{\mathrm{out}},c_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}]=\delta(c_{\mathrm{out},c}-c_{\mathrm{in},c})\big({\det}^{\prime}_{\Omega^{0}(\Sigma)}\Delta_{g}\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\mathbb{I}(\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}})}.

Here
\mathbb{I}
is given by (
35
).

5.3.2.
Nonabelian case

In the nonabelian Chern–Simons theory with coefficients in a semisimple Lie algebra
\mathcal{G}
(corresponding to a compact group
39
39

39

To simplify the notations, and to be able to write expressions like
g^{-1}\partial g
below, we will assume that
G
is a matrix group. Otherwise, we should use left/right translations in
G
.

G
), the superfield is
\mathcal{A}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(I\times\Sigma,\mathcal{G}[1])
and all the splittings are as before, just with components understood as
\mathcal{G}
-valued forms, paired in the quadratic part of the action via the Killing form
\langle,\rangle
on
\mathcal{G}
. The interaction term of the nonabelian theory, when restricted to the gauge-fixing lagrangian, yields

S_{\mathrm{int}}=\frac{1}{6}\int\langle\mathcal{A},[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}]\rangle=-\int_{\Sigma}\int_{I}dt\langle a^{1,0},\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}a^{0,1}\rangle-\int_{\Sigma}\int_{I}dt\langle c_{\mathrm{fl}},\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}(\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}}+\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{fl}})\rangle.

This adds two new bivalent vertices and a univalent vertex to the Feynman rules.

Let us introduce the following notations:

(59)

\displaystyle F_{+}(x)=\frac{x}{1-e^{-x}}=\sum_{n\geq 0}(-1)^{n}\frac{B_{n}}{n!}x^{n},\quad F_{-}(x)=-\frac{x}{e^{x}-1}=-\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{B_{n}}{n!}x^{n},

(60)

\displaystyle j(\sigma)=\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{B_{n}}{n\cdot n!}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{n}=\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{G}}\log\frac{\sinh\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}{2}}{\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}{2}},

with
B_{n}
the Bernoulli numbers,
B_{0}=1,\;B_{1}=-\frac{1}{2},\;B_{2}=\frac{1}{6},\;B_{3}=0,\;B_{4}=-\frac{1}{30},\ldots

In the following lemma we assume that
\sigma
is in a sufficiently small neighborhood of zero in
\mathcal{G}
, see Remark
5.13
below for details.

Lemma 5.12
.

The partition function of the nonabelian Chern–Simons theory on a cylinder is
Z=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}}}
with the following effective action:

(61)

S^{\mathrm{eff}}=\\
=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}\circ\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}\rangle+\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},\frac{1-e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}}{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}\circ\bar{\partial}\sigma\rangle+\langle\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},\frac{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}-1}{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}\circ\partial\sigma\rangle\\
-\langle\partial\sigma,\frac{e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}+\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}-1}{(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{2}}\circ\bar{\partial}\sigma\rangle-\langle\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}},F_{+}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})\circ c_{\mathrm{out}}+F_{-}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})\circ c_{\mathrm{in}}\rangle-i\hbar\mathbb{W}(\sigma).

In (
61
), the
1
-loop correction
\mathbb{W}
stands for the contribution of “ghost wheels” — cycles of
n\geq 1
ghost-antifield propagators (at the vertices, they interact with the residual field
\sigma
). These graphs are ill-defined in the chosen axial gauge; their formal evaluation yields the expression

(62)

\mathbb{W}(\sigma)=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{B_{n}}{n\cdot n!}\,\mathrm{tr}_{C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\mathcal{G})}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{n}=\mathrm{tr}_{C^{\infty}(\Sigma)}j(\sigma)\cdot

This expression heuristically stands for the “sum over points
z
of
\Sigma
” of
j(\sigma(z))
.

We refer the reader to
[
16
, Section 11.3]
for a one-dimensional toy model of this statement.

Proof.

One has the following classes of Feynman diagrams contributing to the effective action:

Figure 6
.
Feynman diagrams in nonabelian theory on a cylinder with “parallel ghost” polarization.

Here the solid lines represent the “physical propagator” (
56
) and the dashed lines represent the “ghost propagator” (
57
).

These diagrams are calculated easiest by introducing the propagators dressed with
\sigma
-insertions:

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\langle a^{0,1}(t,z)\otimes a^{1,0}(t^{\prime},z^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathrm{dressed}}

\displaystyle=-i\hbar\,\theta(t-t^{\prime})\,\delta^{(2)}(z-z^{\prime})\frac{i}{2}d\bar{z}\,dz^{\prime}\;\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{t^{\prime}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}<t}dt_{1}\cdots dt_{k}(-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{k}

\displaystyle=-i\hbar\,\theta(t-t^{\prime})\,e^{-(t-t^{\prime})\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}\,\delta^{(2)}(z-z^{\prime})\frac{i}{2}d\bar{z}\,dz^{\prime},

\displaystyle\langle c_{\mathrm{fl}}(t,z)

\displaystyle\otimes\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{fl}}(t^{\prime},z^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathrm{dressed}}=-i\hbar\,\delta^{(2)}(z-z^{\prime})\frac{i}{2}dz^{\prime}\,d\bar{z}^{\prime}\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\underbrace{\int_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}\in[0,1]}dt_{1}\cdots dt_{k}\;(\theta(t-t_{1})-t)\,(\theta(t_{1}-t_{2})-t_{1})\cdots(\theta(t_{k}-t^{\prime})-t_{k})}_{\begin{cases}\frac{B_{k+1}(1-t^{\prime})-B_{k+1}(t-t^{\prime})}{(k+1)!},\;\;t>t^{\prime}\\
(-1)^{k}\frac{B_{k+1}(t^{\prime}-t)-B_{k+1}(t^{\prime})}{(k+1)!},\;\;t<t^{\prime}\end{cases}}\;(-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{k}

\displaystyle=-i\hbar\,\delta^{(2)}(z-z^{\prime})\frac{i}{2}dz^{\prime}\,d\bar{z}^{\prime}\cdot\frac{e^{(t^{\prime}-t+\theta(t-t^{\prime}))\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}-e^{t^{\prime}\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}}{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}-1}.

Here
B_{k}(t)
are the Bernoulli polynomials.

Computing the tree Feynman diagrams (i)–(vi) in Figure
6
, we have the following.

\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\big|_{t=1},c_{\mathrm{out}}\rangle\vbox to14.99998pt{}{}-\int_{\Sigma}\langle A^{*}_{\mathrm{res}},c_{\mathrm{out}}\rangle\\
=-\int_{\Sigma}\langle\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}},\int_{0}^{1}dt\,\frac{e^{(1-t)\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}-e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}}{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}-1}\circ c_{\mathrm{out}}\rangle-\int_{\Sigma}\langle A^{*}_{\mathrm{res}},c_{\mathrm{out}}\rangle\\
=-\int_{\Sigma}\langle\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{*},\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}{1-e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}}\circ c_{\mathrm{out}}\rangle
.

(vi)

),

.

Thus, the Feynman diagrams (i)-(vi) in Figure
6
yield the
O(\hbar^{0})
part of the answer (
61
).

Next, consider the one-loop graphs in Figure
6
.
The “physical wheels” — diagrams (vii) — vanish due to the form of the propagator (
56
): they are proportional to

\int_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}\in[0,1]}dt_{1}\cdots dt_{k}\,\theta(t_{1}-t_{2})\theta(t_{2}-t_{3})\cdots\theta(t_{k-1}-t_{k})\theta(t_{k}-t_{1})=0.

Finally, consider the “ghost wheels” — diagrams (viii). The propagator (
57
) is the integral kernel of an operator
K^{\mathrm{gh}}=\mathrm{id}\otimes K^{\mathrm{gh}}_{I}
acting on
C^{\infty}(\Sigma)\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}(I)
with
K^{\mathrm{gh}}_{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}f(t)+dt\,g(t)\mapsto\int_{0}^{1}dt^{\prime}\,(\theta(t-t^{\prime})-t)\,g(t^{\prime})
. As a regularization, let us replace
C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
with
C^{\infty}(X)
, with
X
a finite set of points — the set of vertices of some triangulation of the surface
\Sigma
. In particular,
C^{\infty}(X)
is a finite-dimensional vector space.
Then, the regularized value of the ghost wheel diagram (viii) with
k

\sigma
-insertions is the supertrace:

-i\hbar\;\mathrm{str}_{C^{\infty}(X)\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}(I,\mathcal{G})}(-K^{\mathrm{gh}}\,dt\,\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{k}=-i\hbar\,\mathrm{tr}_{C^{\infty}(X)}\,\mathrm{str}_{\Omega^{\bullet}(I,\mathcal{G})}(-K^{\mathrm{gh}}_{I}\,dt\,\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{k}.

For the supertrace over the interval, we have (see, e.g.,
[
35
]
):

\mathrm{str}_{\Omega^{\bullet}(I,\mathcal{G})}(-K^{\mathrm{gh}}_{I}\,dt\,\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{k}\\
=\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{G}}\int_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}\in[0,1]}(\theta(t_{1}-t_{2})-t_{1})dt_{2}\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}\cdots(\theta(t_{k-1}-t_{k})-t_{k-1})dt_{k}\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}(\theta(t_{k}-t_{1})-t_{1})dt_{1}\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}\\
=\frac{B_{k}}{k!}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{k}.

Summing over the values of
k\geq 1
and taking into account the symmetric factor
1/k
(due to the automorphisms of the wheel graph), we obtain

\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{1}{k}\mathrm{str}_{\Omega^{\bullet}(I,\mathcal{G})}(-K^{\mathrm{gh}}_{I}\,dt\,\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{k}=j(\sigma),

with
j
as in (
60
). Thus, finally, the total contribution of graphs (viii) to the effective action is

-i\hbar\,\mathbb{W}=-i\hbar\,\mathrm{tr}_{C^{\infty}(X)}j(\sigma)=-i\hbar\sum_{z\in X}j(\sigma(z)).

Trying to pass to a limit of dense triangulation
X
obviously leads to an ill-defined result here.

=i\hbar\sum_{z\in X}\int_{I}dt_{1}\cdots\int_{I}dt_{k}(\theta(t_{1}-t_{2})-t_{1})\cdots(\theta(t_{k-1}-t_{k})-t_{k-1})(\theta(t_{k}-t_{1})-t_{k})\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{G}}(-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{k}\\
=-i\hbar\,\frac{B_{k}}{k!}\sum_{z\in X}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma(z)})^{k}.

where the contractions are the nondressed propagators (
57
) with the delta form in
z
replaced with Kronecker symbol
\delta_{zz^{\prime}}
. In this regularized setup we understand the fields
c_{\mathrm{fl}},\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{fl}},\sigma
as supported at the vertices of
X
; fields
c_{\mathrm{fl}},\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{fl}}
also depend on
t\in[0,1]
.
∎

Remark 5.13
.

In Lemma
5.12
we assumed that the residual field
\sigma
takes values in a sufficiently small neighborhood of zero in
\mathcal{G}
, so that the sums of Feynman diagrams in Figure
6
converge.
40
40

40

Curiously, the issue of convergence arises only in diagrams (v), (vi), (viii) — the diagrams involving ghosts.
In fact, they converge iff
\sigma
is valued in
B_{0}\subset\mathcal{G}
where
B_{0}
is
the connected component of the origin in

\left\{\sigma\in\mathcal{G}\,\big|\,{\det}_{\mathcal{G}}\frac{\sinh\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}{2}}{\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}{2}}\neq 0\right\}\qquad\subset\quad\mathcal{G}.

In other words,
B_{0}
is
the subset of
\mathcal{G}
where all eigenvalues of
\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}
lie in the open interval
(-2\pi i,2\pi i)\subset i\mathbb{R}
. Thus, we are assuming that
\sigma
takes values in
B_{0}\subset\mathcal{G}
(cf. the discussion of the Gribov region in the context of 2D Yang-Mills in
[
30
, Section 2.4.1]
). Furthermore, note that the exponential map
\exp\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{G}\rightarrow G
is a diffeomorphism from
B_{0}
onto its image
\exp(B_{0})
. Moreover,
\exp(B_{0})
is an open dense subset of
G
.

5.3.3.
Group-valued parametrization of the residual field

Let us parametrize the residual field
\sigma
by a group-valued map
g=e^{-\sigma}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Sigma\rightarrow G
.

Lemma 5.14
.

The effective action (
61
) can be rewritten as

(63)

S^{\mathrm{eff}}=\int_{\Sigma}\Big(\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},g\,\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}g^{-1}\rangle-\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},\bar{\partial}g\cdot g^{-1}\rangle-\langle\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},g^{-1}\,\partial g\rangle\\
+\langle\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}},F_{-}(\mathrm{ad}_{\log g})\circ c_{\mathrm{out}}+F_{+}(\mathrm{ad}_{\log g})\circ c_{\mathrm{in}}\rangle\Big)+\mathrm{WZW}(g)-i\hbar\mathbb{W}.

Here

(64)

\mathrm{WZW}(g)=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\langle\partial g\cdot g^{-1},\bar{\partial}g\cdot g^{-1}\rangle-\frac{1}{12}\int_{I\times\Sigma}\langle d\widetilde{g}\cdot\widetilde{g}^{-1},[d\widetilde{g}\cdot\widetilde{g}^{-1},d\widetilde{g}\cdot\widetilde{g}^{-1}]\rangle.

is the Wess–Zumino–Witten action, where
\widetilde{g}=e^{(t-1)\sigma}
is the extension of
g
to a mapping
I\times\Sigma\rightarrow G
, interpolating between
\widetilde{g}=g
at
t=0
and
\widetilde{g}=1
at
t=1
.
41
41

41

By the standard result on Wess–Zumino terms,
\mathrm{WZW}(g)\bmod 4\pi^{2}\mathbb{Z}
is independent of the choice of
\widetilde{g}
interpolating between
g
on one end of the cylinder and
1
on the other.

Remark 5.15
.

Under the convergence assumption that
\sigma
is valued in
B_{0}
(see Remark
5.13
), or equivalently that
g
is valued in
\exp(B_{0})
— a contractible open dense subset of
G
,
\mathrm{WZW}(g)
is a single-valued function of
g
, and hence
S^{\mathrm{eff}}
is also a single-valued expression. If
g
is allowed to roam the entire group
G
,
\mathrm{WZW}(g)
(and thus
S^{\mathrm{eff}}
) becomes multi-valued, defined only
\bmod\,4\pi^{2}\mathbb{Z}
. In the latter case, for
e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}}}
to be a single-valued expression, one needs
\hbar=\frac{2\pi}{k}
with
k\in\mathbb{Z}
an integer level. The fact that quantization of
\hbar
is necessary in one case but not in the other can be traced to the fact that the Cartan
3
-form (whose pullback by
\widetilde{g}
is the integrand in the second term in the r.h.s. of (
64
)) represents a nontrivial cohomology class on
G
but is exact when restricted to
\exp(B_{0})
.

Proof of Lemma
5.14
.

First terms in (
61
) and (
63
) obviously match.
We have

\displaystyle g^{-1}\partial g=e^{\sigma}\int_{0}^{1}d\tau\,e^{-\tau\sigma}(-\partial\sigma)e^{-(1-\tau)\sigma}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}d\tau\,e^{\tau\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}(-\partial\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}-1}{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}(-\partial\sigma),

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}g\cdot g^{-1}=\int_{0}^{1}d\tau\,e^{-\tau\sigma}(-\bar{\partial}\sigma)e^{-(1-\tau)\sigma}e^{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}d\tau\,e^{-\tau\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}(-\bar{\partial}\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}}{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}(-\bar{\partial}\sigma).

Thus, second and third terms in (
61
) and (
63
) also match. Next, evaluating the Wess-Zumino term on our preferred extension
\widetilde{g}=e^{(t-1)\sigma}
, we have

(65)

-\frac{1}{12}\int_{I\times\Sigma}\langle d\widetilde{g}\cdot\widetilde{g}^{-1},[d\widetilde{g}\cdot\widetilde{g}^{-1},d\widetilde{g}\cdot\widetilde{g}^{-1}]\rangle\\
=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{0}^{1}dt\Big\langle\sigma,\int_{0}^{1-t}d\tau\int_{0}^{1-t}d\tau^{\prime}\Big[e^{-\tau\sigma}(-d\sigma)e^{-(1-t-\tau)\sigma}e^{(1-t)\sigma},e^{-\tau^{\prime}\sigma}(-d\sigma)e^{-(1-t-\tau^{\prime})\sigma}e^{(1-t)\sigma}\Big]\Big\rangle\\
=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{0}^{1}dt\int_{0}^{1-t}d\tau\int_{0}^{1-t}d\tau^{\prime}\Big\langle d\sigma,\Big[\sigma,e^{(\tau^{\prime}-\tau)\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}d\sigma\Big]\Big\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\Big\langle d\sigma,\Big[\sigma,\Big(\frac{\sinh\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}{(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{3}}\Big)\,d\sigma\Big]\Big\rangle\\
=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\Big\langle d\sigma,\Big(\frac{\sinh\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}{(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{2}}\Big)\,d\sigma\Big\rangle.

The WZW kinetic term is:

(66)

-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\langle\partial g\cdot g^{-1},\bar{\partial}g\cdot g^{-1}\rangle\\
=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{0}^{1}d\tau\int_{0}^{1}d\tau^{\prime}\Big\langle e^{-\tau\sigma}(-\partial\sigma)e^{-(1-\tau)\sigma}e^{\sigma},e^{-\tau^{\prime}\sigma}(-\bar{\partial}\sigma)e^{-(1-\tau^{\prime})\sigma}e^{\sigma}\Big\rangle\\
=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{0}^{1}d\tau\int_{0}^{1}d\tau^{\prime}\Big\langle\partial\sigma,e^{(\tau-\tau^{\prime})\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}\bar{\partial}\sigma\Big\rangle=-\int_{\Sigma}\Big\langle\partial\sigma,\frac{\cosh\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}-1}{(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{2}}\bar{\partial}\sigma\Big\rangle.

Putting the kinetic term (
66
) and the Wess-Zumino term (
65
) together, we obtain

\mathrm{WZW}(g)=\int_{\Sigma}-\Big\langle\partial\sigma,\frac{\cosh\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}-1}{(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{2}}\bar{\partial}\sigma\Big\rangle+\Big\langle\partial\sigma,\frac{\sinh\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}{(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{2}}\,\bar{\partial}\sigma\Big\rangle\\
=-\int_{\Sigma}\Big\langle\partial\sigma,\frac{e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}+\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}-1}{(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{2}}\,\bar{\partial}\sigma\Big\rangle.

Thus, finally,
\mathrm{WZW}
term in (
63
) coincides with the fourth term in (
61
).

Ghost terms and the 1-loop contributions in (
61
) and (
63
) are identified directly.

∎

5.3.4.
A comment on ghost wheels

To understand the role of the term
\mathbb{W}
(ghost wheels) in (
63
), recall that, for
\mu_{G}
the Haar measure on the group
G
and
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}
the Lebesgue measure on the Lie algebra, one has
\exp^{*}\mu_{G}=e^{j}\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{G}}
, with
j
the function on
\mathcal{G}
defined by the formula (
60
). Therefore, the half-density on the space of residual fields
associated to the effective action (
63
) is, heuristically, the following:
42
42

42

Recall, see
[
38
]
, that on an
(n|n)
-dimensional odd symplectic supermanifold
(\mathcal{M},\omega)
, half-densities can be understood as cohomology classes of the differential
\omega\wedge
acting on differential forms
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})
. Moreover, if
(x^{i},\xi_{i})
are Darboux coordinates,
i=1,\ldots,n
, with
x^{i}
the even coordinates, then each cohomology class has a unique representative of the form
\rho(x,\xi)dx^{1}\cdots dx^{n}\in\Omega^{n}(\mathcal{M})
corresponding to the half-density
\rho(x,\xi)\prod_{i}d^{\frac{1}{2}}xD^{\frac{1}{2}}\xi
, with
\rho
some function. In (
67
), “
\sim
” refers this choice of preferred representative for a half-density.

(67)

e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}}}\mathcal{D}^{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma\;\mathcal{D}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}}\sim e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}}}\mathcal{D}\sigma\\
=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}}\;\;``\prod_{z\in\Sigma}\underbrace{e^{j(\sigma(z))}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}(\sigma(z))}_{\mu_{G}(g(z))}"=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}}\;\mathcal{D}g.

Here
S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}
stands for (
63
) without the
\mathbb{W}
term
43
43

43

The superscript
(0)
means the “
0
-loop part,” corresponding to the expansion in powers of
\hbar
:
S^{\mathrm{eff}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-i\hbar)^{k}S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(k)}
. In the present case, we have only
k=0,1
terms.

— the latter was used in transforming the functional measure from the pointwise product of Lebesgue measures for
\sigma
to the product of Haar measures for
g
. The equivalence (
67
) of a half-densities is an extension of a rigorous result presented in
[
16
]
for a finite-dimensional system.

The odd symplectic form on residual fields is

(68)

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{res}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\delta\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}},\delta\sigma\rangle=\delta\int_{\Sigma}\langle\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}},\delta\sigma\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta\int_{\Sigma}\langle\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}},-\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}{1-e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}}\circ(\delta g\cdot g^{-1})\rangle=\delta\int_{\Sigma}\langle-\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}-1}\circ\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}},\delta g\cdot g^{-1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\delta g^{*},\delta g\rangle,

where we introduced the notation

(69)

g^{*}=-g^{-1}\cdot\big(F_{+}(\mathrm{ad}_{\log g})\circ\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}}\big)=\big(F_{-}(\mathrm{ad}_{\log g})\circ\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}}\big)\cdot g^{-1}

— a reparametrization of the residual field
\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}}
such that
(g,g^{*})
form Darboux coordinates on
\mathcal{V}
.

Rewritten in terms of the parametrization
(g,g^{*})
for residual fields, the half-density (
67
) becomes

(70)

e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}}}\mathcal{D}^{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma\;\mathcal{D}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}}=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}}\mathcal{D}^{\frac{1}{2}}g\;\mathcal{D}^{\frac{1}{2}}g^{*}.

I.e., in the
(g,g^{*})
-parametrization, the ghost loops go away and the effective action has no quantum corrections.

Remark 5.16
.

In the context of BV formalism, it is natural to think of
S^{\mathrm{eff}}
as a “log-half-density” (see, e.g.,
[
35
, section 2.6]
) on the space of residual fields, rather than a function, i.e., behaving under a change of Darboux coordinates as

S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{[x,\xi]}(x,\xi)=S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{[x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}]}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})-i\hbar\log\mathrm{sdet}\frac{\partial(x,\xi)}{\partial(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})},

so that one has
e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{[x,\xi]}(x,\xi)}d^{\frac{1}{2}}xD^{\frac{1}{2}}\xi=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{[x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}]}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})}d^{\frac{1}{2}}x^{\prime}D^{\frac{1}{2}}\xi^{\prime}
. Here the superdeterminant (Berezinian)
\mathrm{sdet}\cdots
is the Jacobian of the transformation. With that in mind, the effective action (
61
) is
S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{[\sigma,\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}}]}
— relative to the coordinate system
(\sigma,\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}})
on
\mathcal{V}
. On the other hand,
S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}
given by (
63
) without the
-i\hbar\mathbb{W}
term is
S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{[g,g^{*}]}
— relative to the coordinate system
(g,g^{*})
.

5.3.5.
Effective action vs. Hamilton–Jacobi

Denote

(71)

\mathbb{I}(\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}};g)=\\
=\int_{\Sigma}\Big(\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},g\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}g^{-1}\rangle-\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},\bar{\partial}g\cdot g^{-1}\rangle-\langle\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},g^{-1}\partial g\rangle\Big)+\mathrm{WZW}(g)

— the effective action (
63
) restricted to fields of ghost number zero and without the
O(\hbar)
term.

Function (
71
) produces, as a generalized generating function (see
[
16
, Appendix A]
and Section
2
),
with
g
an auxiliary parameter, the following lagrangian
L\subset\overline{\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\mathrm{in}}}\times\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\mathrm{out}}
in the phase space for the boundary of the cylinder:

(72)

\displaystyle L=\Big\{

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}|_{t=1}=\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{out}}^{1,0}+\underbrace{g\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}g^{-1}-\bar{\partial}g\cdot g^{-1}}_{\mathsf{A}^{0,1}\big|_{t=1}=\frac{\delta\mathbb{I}}{\delta\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}}}\;,

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}|_{t=0}=\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{in}}^{0,1}+\underbrace{g^{-1}\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}g+g^{-1}\cdot\partial g}_{\mathsf{A}^{1,0}\big|_{t=0}=-\frac{\delta\mathbb{I}}{\delta\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}}}\;\;\Big|\;\;Y=0\Big\},

where we denoted
44
44

44

The notation
Y=-\frac{\delta\mathbb{I}}{\delta g\cdot g^{-1}}
means that variation of
\mathbb{I}
under the variation of
g
is
\delta_{g}\mathbb{I}=-\int_{\Sigma}\langle\delta g\cdot g^{-1},Y\rangle
.

(73)

Y=-\frac{\delta\mathbb{I}}{\delta g\cdot g^{-1}}=\\
=[\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{out}}^{1,0},g\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}g^{-1}]+(\bar{\partial}-\mathrm{ad}_{\bar{\partial}g\cdot g^{-1}})\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{out}}^{1,0}+\partial(g\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}g^{-1})-\partial(\bar{\partial}g\cdot g^{-1}).

Lemma 5.17
.

The lagrangian (
72
) generated by the functional (
71
) — the tree part of the effective action, restricted to
\mathrm{gh}=0
fields — coincides with the evolution relation
in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\mathrm{in}}}\times\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\mathrm{out}}

for
Chern–Simons theory on the cylinder
I\times\Sigma
.

Proof.

We are restricting our attention only the to
\mathrm{gh}=0
connection field
\mathsf{A}+dt\cdot a
with
\mathsf{A}
a
t
-dependent
1
-form on
\Sigma
and
a
a
t
-dependent
0
-form on
\Sigma
(both are
\mathcal{G}
-valued). The equation of motion — zero-curvature condition —
F_{\mathsf{A}+dt\,a}=0
splits into

(74)

\displaystyle d_{\Sigma}\mathsf{A}+\frac{1}{2}[\mathsf{A},\mathsf{A}]=0,

(75)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathsf{A}=(d_{\Sigma}+[\mathsf{A},-])a.

Equation (
75
) says that
\mathsf{A}
changes by a continuous gauge transformation on
\Sigma
as
t
changes, with
a
the infinitesimal generator. Thus,

(76)

\mathsf{A}|_{t=1}=g\,\mathsf{A}|_{t=0}\,g^{-1}+gd_{\Sigma}g^{-1},\quad\mathrm{with}\;\;g=P\overleftarrow{\exp}\left(-\int_{0}^{1}dt\,a\right).

This implies that we can recover the
(1,0)
component of
\mathsf{A}
at
t=0
from its known value at
t=1
and can recover the
(0,1)
component at
t=1
from its known value at
t=0
. Thus,

(77)

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}|_{t=1}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}+\mathsf{A}^{0,1}|_{t=1}=\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}+g\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}g^{-1}+g\bar{\partial}g^{-1},

(78)

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}+\mathsf{A}^{1,0}|_{t=0}=\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}+g^{-1}\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}g+g^{-1}\partial g.

Note that these two equations coincide with the first two equations in (
72
). Next, equation (
74
) means that the curvature of
\mathsf{A}
must vanish on
\Sigma\times\{t\}
for any
t
. In fact, it suffices to verify it just for one value of
t
, because for all others it would follow from (
75
). Checking (
74
) at
t=1
, we have

(79)

F_{\mathsf{A}}\big|_{t=1}=\underbrace{\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}+\partial(g\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}g^{-1}-\bar{\partial}g\cdot g^{-1})}_{d_{\Sigma}\mathsf{A}|_{t=1}}+[\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},g\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}g^{-1}-\bar{\partial}g\cdot g^{-1}]\quad=0.

This equation coincides with the constraint
Y=0
in (
72
) coming from equating to zero the variation of the generating function
\mathbb{I}
in the auxiliary parameter
g
.

Thus, we have checked that the lagrangian in the boundary phase space induced from the equations of motion (the evolution relation) coincides with the lagrangian generated by
\mathbb{I}
. ∎

Remark 5.18
.

The function
\mathbb{I}
given by (
71
) is also the Hamilton–Jacobi action (see
[
16
, Section 7.2]
): it is the evaluation of the Chern–Simons action with polarization terms, restricted to degree-zero fields,

S^{f}_{\mathrm{ph}}=\int_{I\times\Sigma}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\langle A,dA\rangle+\frac{1}{6}\langle A,[A,A]\rangle\Big)+\int_{\{1\}\times\Sigma}\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0},\mathsf{A}^{0,1}\rangle-\int_{\{0\}\times\Sigma}\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{A}^{0,1},\mathsf{A}^{1,0}\rangle,

on any connection
1
-form
A

solving the “evolution equation”
\iota_{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}}F_{A}=0

subject to boundary conditions
(A|_{t=1})^{1,0}=\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}
,
(A|_{t=0})^{0,1}=\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}
and with the parallel transport of
A
along the interval
I\times\{z\}
given by
g(z)\in G
for any
z\in\Sigma
. One proves this by an explicit computation similar to the proof of Lemma
5.14
, picking a convenient gauge equivalent representative for
A=\mathsf{A}+a\,dt
with
a
constant along
I
(but allowed to vary in
\Sigma
direction). Here we are using gauge-invariance of Chern–Simons action
\bmod\,4\pi^{2}\mathbb{Z}
with respect to gauge transformations trivial on the boundary.

5.3.6.
Quantum master equation

Quantum BFV operators on in- and out-states
\Omega_{\mathrm{in}},\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}
are given by canonical quantization of the boundary BFV action

S^{\mathrm{BFV}}_{\Sigma}=\pm\int_{\Sigma}\langle c,F_{\mathsf{A}}\rangle+\langle\mathsf{A}^{*},\frac{1}{2}[c,c]\rangle

with
\pm
corresponding to out-/in-boundary. Explicitly, quantum BFV operators are
45
45

45

The BFV operator (
81
), its generalization to the case of Wilson lines intersecting the boundary — see (
95
) below — and its cohomology in genus zero were discussed in
[
1
]
.

(80)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\left\langle c_{\mathrm{out}},\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}-i\hbar(\partial+[\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},-])\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}}\right\rangle-i\hbar\left\langle\frac{1}{2}[c_{\mathrm{out}},c_{\mathrm{out}}],\frac{\delta}{\delta c_{\mathrm{out}}}\right\rangle,

(81)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\left\langle c_{\mathrm{in}},-\partial\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}-i\hbar(\bar{\partial}+[\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},-])\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}}\right\rangle-i\hbar\left\langle\frac{1}{2}[c_{\mathrm{in}},c_{\mathrm{in}}],\frac{\delta}{\delta c_{\mathrm{in}}}\right\rangle.

Lemma 5.19
.

The partition function
Z=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}}}
with
S^{\mathrm{eff}}
given by (
61
), (
63
) satisfies the modified quantum master equation

(82)

(\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}+\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathrm{res}})Z=0

with
\Delta_{\mathrm{res}}=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\frac{\delta}{\delta\sigma},\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}}}\rangle
the BV Laplacian on residual fields.

Proof.

Given the ansatz
Z=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}}}
, the equation (
82
) can be written as

(83)

Z^{-1}\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}Z+Z^{-1}\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}Z+\frac{1}{2}\{S^{\mathrm{eff}},S^{\mathrm{eff}}\}_{\mathrm{res}}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathrm{res}}S^{\mathrm{eff}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}0

with
\{,\}_{\mathrm{res}}
the odd Poisson bracket on residual fields associated with the symplectic structure (
68
). Moreover, using the decomposition
S^{\mathrm{eff}}=S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}-i\hbar\,\mathbb{W}(\sigma)
, the mQME can be further rewritten as

(84)

Z^{-1}\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}Z+Z^{-1}\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}Z+\frac{1}{2}\{S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)},S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}\}_{\mathrm{res}}-\\
-i\hbar\Big(\{S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)},\mathbb{W}\}_{\mathrm{res}}+\Delta_{\mathrm{res}}S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}0.

It is easiest to compute the term
\frac{1}{2}\{S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)},S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}\}_{\mathrm{res}}
using
(g,g^{*})
- parametrization of residual fields. We have

\displaystyle S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}+\int_{\Sigma}\langle g^{*},c_{\mathrm{out}}g-gc_{\mathrm{in}}\rangle,

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{\delta g}S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-g^{-1}Y+g^{*}c_{\mathrm{out}}+c_{\mathrm{in}}g^{*},

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{\delta g^{*}}S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{\mathrm{out}}g-gc_{\mathrm{in}},

with
\mathbb{I}
as in (
71
) and
Y
as in (
73
). Thus,

\frac{1}{2}\{S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)},S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}\}_{\mathrm{res}}=S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}\left(\int_{\Sigma}\langle\frac{\overleftarrow{\delta}}{\delta g},\frac{\overrightarrow{\delta}}{\delta g^{*}}\rangle\right)S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}\\
=\int_{\Sigma}\langle c_{\mathrm{out}}-gc_{\mathrm{in}}g^{-1},-Y+gg^{*}c_{\mathrm{out}}+gc_{\mathrm{in}}g^{*}\rangle.

Acting on the partition function with the boundary BFV operators yields

\displaystyle Z^{-1}\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}Z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}\Big\langle c_{\mathrm{out}},\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}+(\partial+[\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},-])(g\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}g^{-1}-\bar{\partial}g\;g^{-1})\Big\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle[c_{\mathrm{out}},c_{\mathrm{out}}],gg^{*}\rangle,

\displaystyle Z^{-1}\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}Z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}\Big\langle c_{\mathrm{in}},-\partial\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}-(\bar{\partial}+[\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},-])(g^{-1}\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}g+g^{-1}\partial g)\Big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle[c_{\mathrm{in}},c_{\mathrm{in}}],g^{*}g\rangle.

Putting together these computations, we find that

(85)

Z^{-1}\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}Z+Z^{-1}\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}Z+\frac{1}{2}\{S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)},S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}\}_{\mathrm{res}}=0

— all the terms in this combination cancel out. This gives us the mQME in the leading order
O(\hbar^{0})
.

For the two remaining terms,
\{S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)},\mathbb{W}\}_{\mathrm{res}}
and
\Delta_{\mathrm{res}}S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}
, we will use the
(\sigma,\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}})
-parametrization for residual fields. The variation of
j(\sigma)
(see (
60
)) in
\sigma
is

\delta_{\sigma}j(\sigma)=\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{G}}P(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})\mathrm{ad}_{\delta\sigma},\qquad\mathrm{where}\quad P(x)=\frac{1}{2}\coth\frac{x}{2}-\frac{1}{x}.

Therefore, using (
62
), we have

(86)

\displaystyle\{S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)},\mathbb{W}\}_{\mathrm{res}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\Sigma}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{G}}P(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})[-F_{+}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})\circ c_{\mathrm{out}}-F_{-}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})\circ c_{\mathrm{in}},\;\bullet]

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\Sigma}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{G}}P(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})[-c_{\mathrm{out}}+c_{\mathrm{in}},\;\bullet].

Here the last simplification relies on the identity

(87)

\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{G}}[(\mathrm{ad}_{x})^{a}\circ y,(\mathrm{ad}_{x})^{b}\bullet]=0\qquad\mathrm{for}\;\;a\geq 1,\;b\geq 0,

which follows from the cyclic property of the trace and Jacobi identity. We also have

(88)

\Delta_{\mathrm{res}}S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}=\sum_{z\in\Sigma}-\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{G}}\sum_{r,s\geq 0}\frac{B_{r+s+1}}{(r+s+1)!}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{r}\mathrm{ad}_{\bullet}\Big((-1)^{r+s+1}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{s}c_{\mathrm{out}}-(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{s}c_{\mathrm{in}}\Big)\\
=\sum_{z\in\Sigma}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{G}}\sum_{r,s\geq 0}\frac{B_{r+s+1}}{(r+s+1)!}\Big[(-1)^{r+s+1}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{s}c_{\mathrm{out}}-(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{s}c_{\mathrm{in}},(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{r}\bullet\Big]\\
\underset{(\ref {trace identity})}{=}\sum_{z\in\Sigma}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{G}}\sum_{r\geq 0}\frac{B_{r+1}}{(r+1)!}[c_{\mathrm{out}}-c_{\mathrm{in}},(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{r}\bullet]=\sum_{z\in\Sigma}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{G}}\;\mathrm{ad}_{c_{\mathrm{out}}-c_{\mathrm{in}}}\circ P(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}).

Comparing (
86
) and (
88
), we see that they exactly cancel each other pointwise on
\Sigma
. Thus,

\{S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)},\mathbb{W}\}_{\mathrm{res}}+\Delta_{\mathrm{res}}S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}=0.

Together with (
85
), this finishes the proof of mQME (
84
).
∎

Remark 5.20
.

The check of the mQME above clearly breaks into two parts:

(a)

The classical part

(89)

Z^{-1}\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}Z+Z^{-1}\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}Z+\frac{1}{2}\{S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)},S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}\}=0,

which is unambiguous and requires no regularization.

(b)

The quantum part

\{S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)},\mathbb{W}\}_{\mathrm{res}}+\Delta_{\mathrm{res}}S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}=0,

which makes sense with the same regularization as the one used in the proof of Lemma
5.12
: replacing
\Sigma
with the set of vertices of a triangulation.

Aside: mQME and Polyakov-Wiegmann formula.

The classical part of the mQME, equation (
89
), itself splits into two parts: terms involving the antifield
g^{*}
and others. The terms involving
g^{*}
cancel due to invariance of the inner product. The cancellation of the remaining terms can be understood in terms of the WZW model as follows. The part of the effective action
\mathbb{I}(\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}};g)
defined in (
71
) can be identified as the WZW action coupled to two external chiral gauge fields
\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}
, see, e.g., eq. (4.5) in
[
23
, §4.2]
. This coupling is sometimes called “gauging the
G_{L}\times G_{R}
symmetry”, for instance in
[
42
]
. For us it is more natural to call it the
G_{\mathrm{in}}\times G_{\mathrm{out}}
-action. Explicitly, the action of
(h_{\mathrm{in}},h_{\mathrm{out}})\in G\times G
on
(\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}};g)
is

(90)

(h_{\mathrm{in}},h_{\mathrm{out}})\cdot(\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}};g)=\left({}^{h_{\mathrm{in}}}(\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}),{}^{h_{\mathrm{out}}}(\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}});h_{\mathrm{out}}gh_{\mathrm{in}}^{-1}\right).

It is well known that under the transformation (
90
)
\mathbb{I}
is not invariant, but transforms according to the Polyakov–Wiegmann
[
37
]
formula:

(91)

\mathbb{I}\left({}^{h_{\mathrm{in}}}(\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}),{}^{h_{\mathrm{out}}}(\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}});h_{\mathrm{out}}gh_{\mathrm{in}}^{-1}\right)=\mathbb{I}(\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}};g)-\mathbb{I}(\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},0;h_{\mathrm{in}})+\mathbb{I}(0,\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}};h_{\mathrm{out}}).

We claim that this equation is just the finite version of the classical part of the mQME, eq. (
89
) above. To see this, consider a path
(h_{\mathrm{in}}(t),h_{\mathrm{out}}(t))
of gauge transformations starting at the identity and compute the derivative of (
91
) at
t=0
. The computation is quite straightforward and we just sketch it: using eq. (
73
), we get

\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\mathbb{I}\left(\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},h_{\mathrm{out}}(t)gh_{\mathrm{in}}(t)^{-1}\right)=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\dot{h}_{\mathrm{out}}-g\dot{h}_{\mathrm{in}}g^{-1},Y\rangle.

Upon identifying
c_{\mathrm{in}}=\dot{h}_{\mathrm{in}},c_{\mathrm{out}}=\dot{h}_{\mathrm{out}}
this gives the piece of
\frac{1}{2}\{S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)},S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}\}
of (
89
) not involving
g^{*}
. Then, we find that

\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\mathbb{I}\left({}^{h_{\mathrm{in}}(t)}(\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}),\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},g\right)+\mathbb{I}(\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},0,h_{\mathrm{in}}(t))\\
=-\bar{\partial}_{\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}}\dot{h}_{\mathrm{in}}(g^{-1}A^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}g+g^{-1}\partial g)-\int\langle\dot{h}_{\mathrm{in}},Y\big|_{g=1,A^{0,1}_{\mathrm{out}}=0}\rangle=Z^{-1}\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}Z\bigg|_{g^{*}=0,c_{\mathrm{in}}=\dot{h}_{\mathrm{in}},c_{\mathrm{out}}=\dot{h}_{\mathrm{out}}}

and similarly for the action of
h_{\mathrm{out}}
.
Overall, we find

\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\left[\mathbb{I}\left({}^{h_{\mathrm{in}}}(\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}),{}^{h_{\mathrm{out}}}(\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}});h_{\mathrm{out}}gh_{\mathrm{in}}^{-1}\right)+\mathbb{I}(\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},0,h_{\mathrm{in}}(t))-\mathbb{I}(0,\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},h_{\mathrm{out}}(t)\right]\\
=\left[Z^{-1}\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}Z+Z^{-1}\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}Z+\frac{1}{2}\{S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)},S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}\}\right]_{g^{*}=0,c_{\mathrm{in}}=\dot{h}_{\mathrm{in}},c_{\mathrm{out}}=\dot{h}_{\mathrm{out}}},

which proves the claim that (a part of) the
\hbar=0
part of the mQME is equivalent to the infinitesimal Polyakov–Wiegmann formula.
We will comment further on the relationship between Chern–Simons theory on
\Sigma\times I
and WZW theory on
\Sigma
in Section
5.3.8
below.

Remark 5.21
.

In the mQME (
82
) and the proof above we were using the
(g,\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}})
-parametrization of residual fields for the BV Laplacian. The corresponding statement for the BV Laplacian in
(g,g^{*})
-parametrization,

\Delta_{[g,g^{*}]}=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\frac{\delta}{\delta g},\frac{\delta}{\delta g^{*}}\rangle=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\frac{\delta}{\delta g\;g^{-1}},\frac{\delta}{g\,\delta g^{*}}\rangle,

is:

(92)

(\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}+\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{[g,g^{*}]})\;e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}}=0.

Note that here we should not be including the
-i\hbar\mathbb{W}
term in the effective action, cf. Remark
5.16
. The proof of (
92
) is exactly as before in the order
O(\hbar^{0})
. In the order
O(\hbar^{1})
, we have

\Delta_{[g,g^{*}]}S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}=\sum_{z\in\Sigma}\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{div}_{T^{*}[-1]G}\big\{\langle g^{*},c_{\mathrm{out}}g-gc_{\mathrm{in}}\rangle,\bullet\big\}.

The hamiltonian vector field generated by the ghost term in the effective action is the cotangent lift to
T^{*}[-1]G
of the vector field

X=\langle c_{\mathrm{out}},\frac{\partial}{\partial g\;g^{-1}}\rangle-\langle c_{\mathrm{in}},\frac{\partial}{g^{-1}\partial g}\rangle.

— This is a sum of a right-invariant and a left-invariant vector field on
G
. Since the Haar measure is bi-invariant,
X
has divergence zero.
Therefore,

\Delta_{[g,g^{*}]}S^{\mathrm{eff}\,(0)}=\sum_{z\in\Sigma}\mathrm{div}_{G}X=0.

Ultimately, to avoid the ambiguity as to whether we should be including the term
-i\hbar\mathbb{W}
into the partition function or not, we can use the invariant formulation where the partition function is a
half-density
(rather than a function) on residual fields and the BV Laplacian is the canonical BV Laplacian on half-densities. Then the mQME is

(\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}+\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathrm{res}}^{\mathrm{can}})\,Z^{\mathrm{can}}=0,

where
Z^{\mathrm{can}}=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{[x,\xi]}}d^{\frac{1}{2}}xD^{\frac{1}{2}}\xi
. Here
(x,\xi)
can be any Darboux coordinate system on
\mathcal{V}
, e.g.,
(\sigma,\mathsf{A}^{*}_{\mathrm{res}})
or
(g,g^{*})
.

Summary.

Summarizing the main results of Section
5.3
, we have the following:

•

The canonical partition function of the nonabelian theory on the cylinder
[0,1]\times\Sigma
with the “parallel ghost” polarization is:

Z^{\mathrm{can}}=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{[g,g^{*}]}}\mathcal{D}^{\frac{1}{2}}g\,\mathcal{D}^{\frac{1}{2}}g^{*}
where the effective action relative to the coordinate system
(g,g^{*})
on
\mathcal{V}
is given explicitly by

(93)

S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{[g,g^{*}]}=\\
=\int_{\Sigma}\Big(\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},g\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}g^{-1}\rangle-\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},\bar{\partial}g\cdot g^{-1}\rangle-\langle\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},g^{-1}\partial g\rangle\Big)+\mathrm{WZW}(g)\\
+\int_{\Sigma}-\langle c_{\mathrm{out}},g\,g^{*}\rangle+\langle c_{\mathrm{in}},g^{*}g\rangle.

In particular, there are no quantum corrections in
S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{[g,g^{*}]}
.

•

Z^{\mathrm{can}}
satisfies the modified quantum master equation.

•

The restriction of
S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{[g,g^{*}]}
to ghost number zero fields is the Hamilton–Jacobi action, i.e., is the generalized generating function for the evolution relation of the classical theory obtained by evaluating the classical action on a solution of the evolution equations, see Section
2
.

Remark 5.22
.

The relation between 3D nonabelian Chern–Simons theory and the (gauged) WZW model was studied from different angles in the literature. The closest discussion to ours, perhaps, was in
[
11
]
:
G/G
WZW theory was recovered from Chern–Simons on a cylinder, using essentially the same gauge fixing and polarization as the ones we employ. But there are crucial differences in the two approaches. We have an explicit Feynman diagram computation of the partition function and prove the QME and the gauge invariance property at the quantum level. In
[
11
]
, on the other hand, quantum gauge invariance was assumed and was used to evaluate the Chern–Simons partition function.

5.3.7.
“Vertical” Wilson lines

One can enrich Chern–Simons theory with Wilson line observables given classicaly by the parallel transport of the connection field
A
along a curve
\gamma
ending on the boundary; the parallel transport is evaluated in some linear representation
\rho
of
G
on a vector space
R
.

Let us consider a very simple case: several “vertical” Wilson lines with
\gamma_{j}=I\times\{z_{j}\}
connecting the in- and out-boundaries of the cylinder
I\times\Sigma
; here
z_{j}
are a collection of points on
\Sigma
,
j=1,\ldots,n
. We are fixing representations
\rho_{j}
for the Wilson lines, with
R_{j}
the respective representation spaces.

Figure 7
.
Vertical Wilson lines.

Note that for our choice of gauge fixing, we have for the Wilson lines

W_{j}=\rho_{j}(P\overleftarrow{\exp}(-\int_{\gamma_{j}}A))=\rho_{j}(e^{-\sigma(z_{j})})=\rho_{j}(g(z_{j}))\qquad\in\mathrm{End}(R_{j}).

I.e., vertical Wilson lines depend only on the residual fields.

Thus, the partition function of the theory enriched with vertical Wilson lines is:

(94)

Z_{I\times\Sigma,\{\gamma_{j}\}}=Z_{I\times\Sigma}\cdot\bigotimes_{j}\rho_{j}(g(z_{j}))

with
Z_{I\times\Sigma}=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}}}
the partition function without the Wilson lines.

The space of out-states is given by functionals of
\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},c_{\mathrm{out}}
with values in
\bigotimes_{j}R_{j}
, while the space of in-states is given by functionals of
\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},c_{\mathrm{in}}
also with values in
\bigotimes_{j}R_{j}^{*}
.
46
46

46

Our convention is that the partition function is not a homomorphism from in-states to out-states (depending on residual fields), but an element of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{out}}\widehat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{in}}
, i.e., without dualization of the in-factor. In this description, gluing of two cylinders involves a pairing between the out-states of the first cylinder
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{out}}^{I}
and the in-states
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{in}}^{II}
of the second.

The BFV operators are:
47
47

47

See
[
1
]
for a construction of these BFV operators from the presentation of Wilson lines via a path integral over auxiliary fields supported on
\gamma_{j}
(the Alekseev–Faddeev–Shatashvili formula).

(95)

\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}^{\Sigma,\{z_{j}\}}=\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}^{\Sigma}+i\hbar\sum_{j}\rho_{j}(c_{\mathrm{out}}(z_{j}))\;\;,\quad\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}^{\Sigma,\{z_{j}\}}=\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}^{\Sigma}+i\hbar\sum_{j}\rho_{j}^{*}(c_{\mathrm{in}}(z_{j})),

where
\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}^{\Sigma}
,
\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}^{\Sigma}
are the BFV operators for the theory without the Wilson lines, given by (
80
), (
81
);
\rho_{j}^{*}
is the dual representation to
\rho_{j}
with representation space
R_{j}^{*}
.

As a direct consequence of Lemma
5.19
, one has that the partition function with Wilson lines (
94
) satisfies the modified quantum master equation:

(\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}^{\Sigma,\{z_{j}\}}+\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}^{\Sigma,\{z_{j}\}}-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathrm{res}})\,Z_{I\times\Sigma,\{\gamma_{j}\}}=0.

Here we understand that the
\rho_{j}^{*}
term in
\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}
acts on the second factor in
\rho_{j}(g(z_{j}))\in R_{j}\otimes R_{j}^{*}
, while the
\rho_{j}
term in
\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}
acts on the first factor.

5.3.8.
The CS-WZW correspondence: WZW theory as an effective theory of Chern–Simons

Equation (
93
) is evidence of a strong relationship between the Chern–Simons theory on a manifold with boundary
\Sigma
and the WZW theory on the Riemann surface
\Sigma
. This relationship has, of course, already been subject to a lot of scrutiny after Witten’s seminal article
[
41
]
. In the approach of this paper, this relationship stems from the fact that the gauged WZW action emerges as an effective action of the Chern–Simons theory, as is clear from eq. (
93
). To be precise, the following two theories are equivalent:

i)

The BV-BFV effective theory of Chern–Simons on a
I\times\Sigma
, restricted to the gauge-fixing lagrangian
\mathcal{L}=\{g^{*}=0\}\subset T^{*}[-1]G
.

ii)

The WZW theory with gauged “
G_{\mathrm{in}}\times G_{\mathrm{out}}
”-symmetry.

This is a very strong statement of equivalence: It means that essentially all quantities computed from the action functional in gauged WZW theory have an expression in Chern–Simons theory. We summarize this relationship in Table
1
below.

Object in CS on
I\times\Sigma

Object in gauged WZW on
\Sigma

Effective action
S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{[g,g*]}

Gauged WZW action
\mathbb{I}(\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}};g)

mQME
(\Omega-\Delta_{\mathrm{res}})Z=0

Polyakov–Wiegmann formula (
91
)

(group 1-cocycle property)

Expectation value
W
of Wilson line

\gamma=I\times\{z\}
in rep.
\rho

Field insertion
\rho(g(z))

Table 1
.
The CS-WZW correspondence

Remark 5.23
.

One might wonder why in Table
1
on the left hand side we have objects defined in the quantization on the Chern--Simons theory, while on the right-hand side we have entirely classical objects in the WZW model. This apparent puzzle is resolved by the observation that on the left-hand side we are seeing only the
semiclassical limit of the quantum Chern--Simons theory (which in this case happens to be exact, since there are no loop contributions).
48
48

48

Up to the subtleties concerning ghost loops discussed above.

Remark 5.24

(Nonequivalent gauge-fixing lagrangians)
.

Instead of
\mathcal{L}=\{g^{*}=0\}
, one could restrict the effective Chern–Simons action (
93
) also to another lagrangian
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\subset T^{*}[-1]G
given by
g=1
. In that way, one obtains

(96)

S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{[g,g^{*}]}\bigg|_{g=1}=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}\rangle+\int_{\Sigma}\langle g^{*},c_{\mathrm{in}}-c_{\mathrm{out}}\rangle.

Upon integrating
Z
over
\mathcal{L}
or
\mathcal{L^{\prime}}
, one obtains two
(\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}+\Omega_{\mathrm{out}})
-cocycles
Z_{1}
,
Z_{2}
.
Z_{1}
is concentrated in ghost degree 0 (we will discuss it in more detail in the next subsection) while

Z_{2}=\exp\left(\frac{i}{\hbar}\int_{\Sigma}\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}\rangle\right)\delta(c_{\mathrm{in}}-c_{\mathrm{out}})

has nonzero ghost number (formally, it is infinite,
\mathrm{gh}=\dim\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{g})
).
Therefore,
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
provide an example of nonequivalent gauge-fixing lagrangians.

In the
(g,g^{*})
-coordinates we can define a particularly simple gauge-fixing lagrangian
\mathcal{L}
given by
g^{*}=0
(for the general remarks in this section, we will allow ourselves to ignore issues arising from possible zero modes).

(97)

S^{\mathrm{eff}}\big|_{\mathcal{L}}=\mathrm{WZW}(g)+\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},g\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}g^{-1}\rangle-\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},\bar{\partial}g\cdot g^{-1}\rangle-\langle\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},g^{-1}\partial g\rangle\equiv\mathbb{I}[\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}};g].

Here
\mathbb{I}[\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}};g]
is the standard way of gauging the WZW action, see, e.g., eq. (4.5) in
[
23
, §4.2]
. We can then express the Chern–Simons partition function on
I\times\Sigma
as

(98)

Z_{I\times\Sigma}[\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}},c_{\mathrm{in}},c_{\mathrm{out}}]\equiv Z_{I\times\Sigma}[\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}]=\int_{g}\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\mathbb{I}[\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}};g]\mathcal{D}g

(notice the partition function does not depend on
c_{\mathrm{in}},c_{\mathrm{out}}
).
This is the definition of the partition function
Z_{\mathsf{A}}^{\mathrm{WZW}}
of gauged WZW, see, e.g., eq. (4.7) in
[
23
, §4.2]
. Here we abbreviate
\mathsf{A}=(\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}})
.
Similarly, we see that a correlator in the gauged WZW theory can be expressed as the partition function of Chern–Simons theory enriched with Wilson lines:

(99)

\langle\rho_{1}(g(z_{1}))\otimes\ldots\otimes\rho_{n}(g(z_{n}))\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\\
=\int_{g}\rho_{1}(g(z_{1}))\otimes\ldots\otimes\rho_{n}(g(z_{n}))e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\mathbb{I}[g,\mathsf{A}]}\mathcal{D}g=\int_{g}Z_{I\times\Sigma,\{\gamma_{j}\}}\mathcal{D}g.

For the purposes of this subsection, we will treat the path integral expressions on the right hand side of (
98
) and (
99
) heuristically. In the literature, these objects are typically defined via representation theory. In this paper, we are typically interpreting path integral expressions as defined via Feynman graphs and rules, but
for WZW the absence of a natural linear structure on the target (the group
G
) obstructs the treatment of the path integral as a perturbed Gaussian integral.
We will therefore simply assume that the partition function exists and defines an element in
\Omega
-cohomology. In
[
1
]
it was shown that in genus 0, the
\Omega
-cohomology with
n
Wilson lines ending on the boundary can be identified with the
n
-point space of conformal blocks. We expect this to hold for all genera, and assume it for the purpose of the next section.
We summarize the content of the CS-WZW correspondence after integrating over
\mathcal{L}
in Table
2
below.

of

5.3.9.
An application: Holomorphic factorization of the WZW theory

We will now discuss an application of the correspondence observed above. The arguments in this section will be more heuristic in nature.

Suppose we fix the boundary condition on one side, e.g., fix the antiholomorphic boundary condition at the
\mathrm{in}
-boundary by setting
\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}=0
(remember that in our treatment of boundary conditions, setting
\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}=0
means that
\mathsf{A}\big|_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\in\Omega^{1,0}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{g})

), and take the
\mathrm{out}
-boundary in the
\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}
-representation. We will call such a cylinder a “chiral cylinder.”

\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{in}}=0
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}

Figure 8
.
A chiral cylinder: antiholomorphic boundary conditions on
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
,
\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}
-representation on
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
. Gray indicates that we fix a boundary condition on this boundary, while hatching indicates we fix only the polarization.

After integrating out
g
, we obtain the partition function
\psi(\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{out}}^{1,0})
of a “chiral gauged WZW theory,” i.e., a WZW theory coupled to a chiral gauge field, see, e.g.,
[
42
]
.
49
49

49

Where Witten suggests that “this in fact can be regarded as the essential relation between the
WZW model and Chern–Simons theory.”
This partition function is not a number, but rather a holomorphic gauge invariant section of a line bundle over the space of connections on
\Sigma
.
50
50

50

In our approach, holomorphicity simply follows from the fact that it depends only on
\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}
, while gauge invariance is the statement that
\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}\psi=0
.
We can glue the chiral cylinder to another “antichiral” cylinder with opposite boundary conditions (see Figure
9
).

*
=

Figure 9
.
Gluing a chiral and an antichiral cylinder into a cylinder with opposite chiral boundary conditions. Gray indicates a fixed boundary condition, hatching indicates a polarized boundary.

In this way, we obtain – as explained in
[
42
]
– the square of the norm of
\psi
.
51
51

51

A missing factor of
\int_{\Sigma}A^{1,0}A^{0,1}
in comparison with
[
42
]
comes from the gluing conventions in BV-BFV, see Appendix
A
.
Here, the “norm square” should be taken with respect to a well-defined inner product on the
\Omega
-cohomology, i.e., the finite-dimensional moduli space of gauge-invariant holomorphic sections.
52
52

52

See, for instance,
[
25
]
for a more detailed discussion in genus 0, or
[
19
]
for a discussion in genus 1. The authors do not know of an explicit construction of this inner product in higher genera.
On the other hand, from the general principles of the BV-BFV formalism, we will then obtain the partition function of Chern–Simons theory with opposite chiral boundary conditions, which is given by specializing to
\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}=\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{in}}=0
in (
98
):

(100)

|\psi|^{2}=Z_{I\times\Sigma}^{\mathrm{CS}}=Z_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{WZW}}.

Here on the left-hand side we have the norm-square of the partition function of chiral WZW, in the middle we have Chern–Simons partition function on the cylinder with opposite chiral boundary conditions, while on the right-hand side we have the definition of the WZW partition function. Equation (
100
) is sometimes called “holomorphic factorization of the WZW model”, because one sees that the partition function of the full WZW model — which does not vary holomorphically on the moduli space of conformal structures — splits into a sum of products of holomorphic and antiholomorphic factors, which do depend (anti)holomorphically on the complex structure. Thus, holomorphic factorization of the WZW model follows from the self-similarity of the Chern–Simons partition function on cylinders.

Using the results of Section
5.3.7
, in particular equation (
94
), these results for the partition function generalize to correlators in chiral and full WZW. Namely, suppose we are given
n
Wilson lines colored by representations
R_{1},\ldots,R_{n}
, and let
V=\otimes_{j}R_{j}
. Then, the Chern–Simons partition function with Wilson lines on a chiral cylinder
\psi_{\{\gamma_{j}\}}
is naturally a degree zero element of
V\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{out}}
with
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{out}}
the space of functionals of
\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},c_{\mathrm{out}}
with values in
V^{*}
. Gluing with an antichiral cylinder, we obtain the Chern–Simons partition function with Wilson lines and opposite boundary conditions - the correlator

\langle\rho_{1}(g(z_{1}))\cdots\rho_{n}(g(z_{n}))\rangle\in V\otimes V^{*}
in a full WZW model. On the other hand, explicitly computing the BV-BFV gluing we obtain
53
53

53

The gluing is defined as a formal functional integral, but its restriction to the finite-dimensional
\Omega
-cohomology gives rise to a well-defined inner product.

(\psi,\overline{\psi})\in V\otimes V^{*}
. Here
(\cdot,\cdot)
is the inner product on the space on
n
-point conformal blocks. Thus, we obtain the generalization of (
100
) to the case with Wilson lines:

(101)

(\psi_{\{\gamma_{j}\}},\overline{\psi}_{\{\gamma_{j}\}})=Z^{CS}_{I\times\Sigma,\{\gamma_{j}\}}=\langle\rho_{1}(g(z_{1}))\cdots\rho_{n}(g(z_{n}))\rangle.

5.4.
3D nonabelian Chern–Simons theory in holomorphic-to-holomorphic polarization

Next, consider the nonabelian Chern–Simons theory on
\Sigma\times[0,1]
with polarizations as in Section
4.1
. The residual fields

\mathsf{A}^{+}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}=\mathsf{A}^{0}_{{I\,\mathrm{res}}}+\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{{I\,\mathrm{res}}},\qquad\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{res}}=\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{res}}+\mathsf{A}^{2}_{\mathrm{res}}

and the gauge fixing are as in Section
4.1
(but now all forms are
\mathcal{G}
-valued). We will use the notations
\sigma=\mathsf{A}^{0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}
,
\lambda=\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{\mathrm{res}}
for
\mathrm{gh}=0
residual fields, as in Section
4.1.1
.

In this subsection we will present only the results; the computations
are similar to those of Section
5.3
.

The Feynman diagrams for the partition function
Z=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}}}
are:

Figure 10
.
Feynman diagrams in nonabelian theory on a cylinder in holomorphic-to-holomorphic polarization.

Here the “physical wheels” (viii) and the “ghost wheels” (ix) cancel each other, due to the form of propagators in the chosen polarization.

Calculating the Feynman diagrams, one finds the following expression for the effective action:

(102)

S^{\mathrm{eff}}=S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathrm{ph}}+S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathrm{gh}},

where the part depending only on “physical” (
\mathrm{gh}=0
) fields (the contribution of diagrams (i), (ii), (iii)) is

(103)

S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathrm{ph}}=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\lambda,\partial\sigma\rangle+\left\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}-1}\circ\lambda+\left(-\frac{1}{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}-1}+\frac{1}{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}\right)\circ\bar{\partial}\sigma\right\rangle\\
+\left\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{in}},-\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}{1-e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}}\circ\lambda+\left(\frac{1}{1-e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}}-\frac{1}{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}\right)\circ\bar{\partial}\sigma\right\rangle

and the ghost-dependent part (the contribution of diagrams (iv)–(vii)) is

(104)

S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathrm{gh}}=\int_{\Sigma}\left\langle c_{\mathrm{out}},\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{B_{k}^{-}}{k!}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{k}\mathsf{A}^{2}_{\mathrm{res}}-\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{B^{-}_{k+1}}{(k+1)!}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{k}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\right.\\
\left.+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}\frac{B^{-}_{k+l+1}}{(k+l+1)!}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{k}\mathrm{ad}_{\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{l}\lambda-\sum_{k,l\geq 0}\frac{B^{-}_{k+l+2}}{(k+l+2)!}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{k}\mathrm{ad}_{\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{l}\bar{\partial}\sigma\right\rangle\\
+\left\langle c_{\mathrm{in}},-\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{B_{k}^{+}}{k!}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{k}\mathsf{A}^{2}_{\mathrm{res}}+\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{B^{+}_{k+1}}{(k+1)!}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{k}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\right.\\
\left.-\sum_{k,l\geq 0}\frac{B^{+}_{k+l+1}}{(k+l+1)!}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{k}\mathrm{ad}_{\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{l}\lambda+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}\frac{B^{+}_{k+l+2}}{(k+l+2)!}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{k}\mathrm{ad}_{\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}}(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})^{l}\bar{\partial}\sigma\right\rangle.

Here
B_{n}^{\pm}
are the Bernoulli numbers with
B^{\pm}_{1}=\pm\frac{1}{2}
and with
B^{+}_{n}=B^{-}_{n}
the usual Bernoulli numbers for
n\neq 1
(thus,
B^{-}_{n}=B_{n}
are the standard Bernoulli numbers for
n=0,1,2,\ldots
):

n

0

1

2

3

4

5

6

\cdots

B^{+}_{n}

1

+\frac{1}{2}

\frac{1}{6}

0

-
\frac{1}{30}

0

\frac{1}{42}

\cdots

B^{-}_{n}

1

-\frac{1}{2}

\frac{1}{6}

0

-
\frac{1}{30}

0

\frac{1}{42}

\cdots

Remark 5.25
.

Another form of the ghost-dependent part of the effective action (
104
), with sums over
k,l
below evaluated explicitly, is:

S^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\mathrm{gh}}=\int_{\Sigma}\Big\langle c_{\mathrm{out}},\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}-1}\circ\mathsf{A}^{2}_{\mathrm{res}}+\Big(-\frac{1}{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}-1}+\frac{1}{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}\Big)\circ\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\\
+\mathrm{ad}_{\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}}\frac{1}{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}-1}\circ\lambda-\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}{1-e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}}\mathrm{ad}\left(\frac{1-e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}}{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}\circ\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\right)\frac{1}{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}-1}\circ\lambda\\
-\frac{1}{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}\mathrm{ad}_{\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}\bar{\partial}\sigma+\frac{1}{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}-1}\mathrm{ad}\left(\frac{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}-1}{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}\circ\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\right)\frac{1}{1-e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}}\circ\bar{\partial}\sigma\Big\rangle\\
+\Big\langle c_{\mathrm{in}},\Big(\sigma\rightarrow-\sigma,\mathsf{A}^{2}_{\mathrm{res}}\rightarrow-\mathsf{A}^{2}_{\mathrm{res}}\Big)\Big\rangle.

Here the coefficient of
c_{\mathrm{in}}
is obtained from the coefficient of
c_{\mathrm{out}}
by replacing
\sigma
with
-\sigma
and replacing
\mathsf{A}^{2}_{\mathrm{res}}
with
-\mathsf{A}^{2}_{\mathrm{res}}
.

Next, one can introduce a new parametrization of the space of residual fields by a group-valued map
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Sigma\rightarrow G
, a
(0,1)
-form
\Lambda
, a
(1,0)
-form
\Lambda^{*}
and a
2
-form
g^{*}
:
54
54

54

\Lambda,\Lambda^{*}
are
\mathcal{G}
-valued forms while
g^{*}
is a
\mathcal{G}
-valued form translated by
g^{-1}
. The ghost number
is
0
for
g,\Lambda
and is
-1
for
g^{*},\Lambda^{*}
.

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=e^{-\sigma},

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle=\mbox{coefficient of $\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}$ in (\ref {hol-hol non-ab S_eff phys part})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}-1}\circ\lambda+\left(-\frac{1}{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}-1}+\frac{1}{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}\right)\circ\bar{\partial}\sigma,

\displaystyle\Lambda^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-e^{-\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}}{\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}}\circ\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}},

\displaystyle g^{*}

\displaystyle=(\mbox{coefficient of $c_{\mathrm{in}}$ in (\ref {hol-hol non-ab S_eff ghost part}) })\cdot g^{-1}.

This change of parametrization has Jacobian
1
and changes one Darboux coordinate system with respect to the BV symplectic form on
\mathcal{V}
into another one:

\omega_{\mathrm{res}}=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\delta\sigma,\delta\mathsf{A}^{2}_{\mathrm{res}}\rangle+\langle\delta\lambda,\delta\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\rangle=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\delta g,\delta g^{*}\rangle+\langle\delta\Lambda,\delta\Lambda^{*}\rangle.

In terms of this new parametrization, the effective action (
102
) can be written more concisely:

(105)

S^{\mathrm{eff}}=-\mathrm{WZW}(g^{-1})-\int_{\Sigma}\langle\Lambda,\partial g\cdot g^{-1}\rangle\\
+\int_{\Sigma}\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}},\Lambda\rangle+\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{in}},g^{-1}\Lambda g+g^{-1}\bar{\partial}g\rangle+\langle c_{\mathrm{out}},-g\,g^{*}+\bar{\partial}\Lambda^{*}+[\Lambda,\Lambda^{*}]\rangle+\langle c_{\mathrm{in}},g^{*}g\rangle,

where
\mathrm{WZW}
is the Wess–Zumino–Witten action defined as in (
64
).

The effective action (
105
)
satisfies the following properties:

•

Its restriction to
\mathrm{gh}=0
fields satisfies the Hamilton–Jacobi property, i.e., it is the generalized generating function for the evolution relation of nonabelian Chern–Simons theory. From this identification one can see that, on-shell,
\Lambda
can be interpreted as the
(0,1)
-component of the connection field at
t=1
.
55
55

55

There is, of course, a similar change of variables where instead we take the
(0,1)
-component at
t=0
to be a coordinate on
\mathcal{V}
. It leads to an expression for
S^{\mathrm{eff}}
where the symmetry between in/out boundaries is broken in the opposite way to (
105
): the coefficients of
\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{in}}
,
c_{\mathrm{out}}
are simple and the coefficients of
\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{\mathrm{out}}
,
c_{\mathrm{in}}
are more complicated.

•

One has the modified quantum master equation

(\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}+\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathrm{res}})e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{\mathrm{eff}}}=0

with the boundary BFV operators

\Omega_{\mathrm{out}/\mathrm{in}}=\int_{\Sigma}\big\langle c,\pm\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,0}-i\hbar(\partial+[\mathsf{A}^{1,0},-])\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathsf{A}^{1,0}}\big\rangle-i\hbar\big\langle\frac{1}{2}[c,c],\frac{\delta}{\delta c}\big\rangle.

Here the sign
\pm
is
+
for out-boundary and
-
for in-boundary;
we also suppressed the
\mathrm{out}/\mathrm{in}
subscript in the boundary fields

\mathsf{A}^{1,0}
and
c
.

6.
BV-BFV approach to higher-dimensional Chern–Simons theories

The observations on abelian Chern–Simons theory in Sections
3
and
4
generalize readily to cylinders
I\times M
of other dimensions
d
. Observe that
d
must be odd because we want the field
\mathcal{A}
to belong to the superspace
\Omega^{\bullet}(I\times M)
or
\Pi\Omega^{\bullet}(I\times M)
and, in either case, the BV action
S=\int_{I\times M}\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}\wedge d\mathcal{A}
is even iff
d
is odd.

In the following, we will actually focus on the graded case where the field
\mathcal{A}
belongs to the graded space
\Omega^{\bullet}(I\times M)[k]
for some integer
k
and the BV action has degree zero. This forces
d=2k+1
.
If
k
were even, we would have
\mathcal{A}\wedge d\mathcal{A}=\frac{1}{2}d\mathcal{A}^{2}
, so the BV action would have no bulk contribution. Therefore, we will have to assume that
k
is odd. To summarize:
56
56

56

There are other ways to get at this results. For example, we may consider the classical theory with action
S_{\text{cl}}=\int_{I\times M}\frac{1}{2}AdA
with
A
a
k
-form for some
k
. This immediately forces
d=2k+1
. For
k
even, we have
AdA=\frac{1}{2}dA^{2}
, so the classical action has no bulk term. We then have to assume
k
odd. Another option is to consider the AKSZ construction with target
\mathbb{R}[k]
, for some
k
