, endowed with a symplectic form. If we denote by
x
the coordinate, the general
2
-form is
\omega=f(x)dxdx
with
f
a function. If
k
is even,
dxdx=0
and
\omega
is degenerate, so we have to assume
k
odd. In this case
f(x)=a+bx
for some real numbers
a
and
b
. Now
\omega
is closed iff
b=0
and nondegenerate iff
a\not=0
(we may, e.g., take
a=\frac{1}{2}
). We then have that
\omega
has degree
2k
. Since we want to produce a BV form (i.e., degree equal to
-1
) on

I\times M
by the AKSZ construction, we need
d=2k+1
.

d=\dim(I\times M)=2k+1,\quad k=2l+1.

The case
k=1
has been considered in Section
4
. We will briefly describe the general case before turning to the next example of interest,
k=3
.

Next we assume that the
2k
-dimensional manifold

M
is closed and oriented. Again, we can construct a BV-BFV theory by the AKSZ construction as

\mathcal{F}=\mathrm{Map}(T[1](I\times M),\mathbb{R}[k])=\Omega^{\bullet}(I\times M)[k]

and rewrite this space of fields in the form

\mathcal{F}=\Omega^{\bullet}(I,\Omega^{\bullet}(M)[k]),

exhibiting the theory as a 1-dimensional Chern–Simons theory with coefficients in
\mathfrak{g}=\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
.
The BV action is then, mimicking (
18
),

S=\int_{I\times M}\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}\wedge d\mathcal{A}=\int_{I}\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{A},d_{I}\mathcal{A})+\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{A},d_{M}\mathcal{A}),

where
d=d_{I}+d_{M}
and
(a,b)=\int_{M}a\wedge b
. Again, the field
\mathcal{A}
can be split as
\mathcal{A}=\mathsf{A}+dt\cdot\mathsf{A}_{I}
and the boundary phase space is
\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{M}=\Omega^{\bullet}(M)[k]
with Noether 1-form

\alpha=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{1\}\times M}\mathsf{A}\wedge\delta\mathsf{A}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{0\}\times M}\mathsf{A}\wedge\delta\mathsf{A}.

Next, assume that
M
carries a complex structure. Then we can split the space of complexified
k
-forms as

\Omega^{k}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)=\bigoplus_{j_{1}+j_{2}=k}\Omega^{j_{1},j_{2}}(M).

Given that
k
is odd, the splitting

(106)

\Omega^{k}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)=\underbrace{\bigoplus_{j=0}^{l}\Omega^{k-j,j}(M)}_{\Omega^{k}_{+}(M)}\oplus\underbrace{\bigoplus_{j=l+1}^{k}\Omega^{k-j,j}(M)}_{\Omega^{k}_{-}(M)}

provides a splitting into lagrangian subspaces of
\Omega^{k}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
(which is the degree 0 subspace of
\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{M}
). The splitting (
20
) generalizes to

\underbrace{\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}}=\underbrace{\bigoplus_{j=0}^{k-1}\Omega^{j}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)\oplus\Omega^{k}_{+}(M)}_{\mathfrak{g}^{+}_{\mathbb{C}}}\oplus\underbrace{\Omega^{k}_{-}(M)\oplus\bigoplus_{j=k+1}^{d-1}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{j}(M)}_{\mathfrak{g}^{-}_{\mathbb{C}}}.

Correspondingly, we split the fields
\mathsf{A}
into its components
\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{A}^{+}+\mathsf{A}^{-}
, and similarly for
\mathsf{A}_{I}
. The de Rham differential restricted to the subcomplex
\Omega^{k-1}(M)\to\Omega^{k}_{+}(M)\oplus\Omega^{k}_{-}(M)\to\Omega^{k+1}(M)
splits as
d_{M}=d_{M}^{+}+d_{M}^{-}
as in Section
5.1
,
57
57

57

In more detail, for a
(p,q)
-form
\alpha
with
p+q=k-1
we have

d_{M}^{+}\alpha=\begin{cases}d_{M}\alpha,&q<l,\\
\partial\alpha,&q=l,\\
0,&q>l.\end{cases}

and vice versa for
d_{M}^{-}
.
see also Figure
11
below.

Figure 11
.
Splitting of
\Omega^{p,q}
into
\mathfrak{g}^{+}
(below the thick line) and
\mathfrak{g}^{-}
(above the thick line), in the case
k=3
. Solid arrows are components of
d^{-}
, dashed arrows are components of
d^{+}
. Horizontal arrows (dashed or not) are
\partial
, vertical ones are
\bar{\partial}
.

In particular, we have
(\mathsf{A}^{+},d_{M}\mathsf{A}^{+})=(\mathsf{A}^{+},d_{M}^{-}\mathsf{A}^{+})
.
Before turning to the particular example of
k=3
, let us briefly have a look at the general form of the partition function in the two polarizations considered already in the last section.

6.1.
Partition functions in
4l+3
-dimensional CS theories

First, let us take
\mathfrak{g}^{+}
as the base of the polarization on both ends of the cylinder. This means that

\mathcal{B}=\mathfrak{g}^{+}[k]\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{+}[k]\ni(\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\rm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\rm{out}})

with fiber

\mathcal{Y}=\Omega^{\bullet}(I,\partial I;\mathfrak{g}^{+}[k])\oplus\Omega^{\bullet}(I,\mathfrak{g}^{-}[k]).

Again, we can gauge fix the polarized theory by choosing

\mathcal{V}=dt\cdot\mathfrak{g}^{+}[k]\oplus 1\cdot\mathfrak{g}^{-}[k]\ni(dt\cdot\mathsf{A}^{+}_{I,\rm{res}},\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\rm{res}})

and using Hodge decomposition (
7
) with chain contraction (
8
) and corresponding propagator (
9
). Thus, we obtain the splitting

\mathcal{A}=\widetilde{\mathsf{A}}_{\rm{in}}^{+}+\widetilde{\mathsf{A}}_{\rm{out}}^{+}+dt\cdot\mathsf{A}^{+}_{I,\rm{res}}+\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\rm{res}}+\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\rm{fl}}+\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\rm{fl}},

where

\widetilde{\mathsf{A}}_{\rm{in}}^{+},\widetilde{\mathsf{A}}_{\rm{out}}^{+}
are the discontinuous extensions of
\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\rm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\rm{out}}
into the bulk. In terms of this splitting, we can rewrite the “perturbation”
\frac{1}{2}\int_{I\times M}\mathcal{A}\wedge d_{M}\mathcal{A}
as

\frac{1}{2}\int_{I\times M}\mathcal{A}\wedge d_{M}\mathcal{A}=\int_{I}dt\int_{M}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{I,\mathrm{res}}d_{M}(\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\rm{res}}+\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\rm{fl}}+\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\rm{fl}}),

since
dt\cdot\mathsf{A}^{+}_{I,\mathrm{res}}
is the only term containing a
dt
. By definition (see (
7
)) the
\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{fl}}
fluctuations have vanishing integral over
I
. Applying the fact that
d_{M}=d_{M}^{+}+d_{M}^{-}
, we obtain

\frac{1}{2}\int_{I\times M}\mathcal{A}\wedge d_{M}\mathcal{A}=\int_{I}dt\left(\int_{M}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{I,\mathrm{res}}d_{M}\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\rm{res}}+\int_{M}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{I,\mathrm{res}}d_{M}^{-}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{fl}}\right).

The BV-BFV partition function - as in (
22
) - is then given by

(107)

Z(\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\rm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\rm{out}},\mathsf{A}^{+}_{I,\rm{res}},\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\rm{res}})=\\
=\int_{\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{K-ex}\subset\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{fl}}\;\mathcal{D}\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{fl}}\;e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{f}\left(\widetilde{\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{in}}}+\widetilde{\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{out}}}+\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{fl}}+\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{res}}+\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{fl}}+dt\cdot\mathsf{A}^{+}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\right)}\\
=\int\mathcal{D}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{fl}}\;\mathcal{D}\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{fl}}\;e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\big(\int_{I\times M}\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{fl}}d_{I}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{fl}}+\int_{\{1\}\times M}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{out}}\mathsf{A}^{-}-\int_{\{0\}\times M}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{in}}\mathsf{A}^{-}+\int_{I\times M}\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}\,d_{M}\mathcal{A}\big)}\\
=\int\mathcal{D}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{fl}}\;\mathcal{D}\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{fl}}\;\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\Big(\int_{I\times M}\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{fl}}\,d_{I}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{fl}}+\int_{M}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{out}}\,(\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{res}}+\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{fl}}\big|_{t=1})-\\
-\int_{M}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{in}}\,(\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{res}}+\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{fl}}\big|_{t=0})+\int_{M}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}d_{M}\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{res}}+\int_{I\times M}dt\;\mathsf{A}^{+}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\,d_{M}^{-}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{fl}}\Big).

This is structurally the same formula as in Section
4
before, with the difference
that the pairing on residual fields in slightly more complicated in this case. The Feynman diagrams defining this functional integral are the same as in (
23
) and yield

Z=\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\int_{M}\Big((\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{out}}-\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{in}})\;\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{res}}+\mathsf{A}^{+}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\,d_{M}\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{res}}+\frac{1}{2}(\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{out}}+\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{in}})\;d_{M}^{-}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\Big).

Similarly, the partition function in the holomorphic-to-antiholomorphic polarization, with space of boundary conditions

\mathcal{B}=\mathfrak{g}^{-}[k]\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{+}[k]\ni(\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{out}})

and space of residual fields

\mathcal{V}=dt\cdot\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}[k-1]\oplus(1-t)\cdot\mathfrak{g}^{+}[k]\oplus t\cdot\mathfrak{g}^{-}[k]\;\;\ni\;\;dt\cdot\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}+(1-t)\cdot\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{res}}+t\cdot\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{res}},

is

\displaystyle Z(\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{out}}

\displaystyle;\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}},\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{res}},\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{res}})

\displaystyle=\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\Bigg(\int_{M}\ -\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{out}}\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{in}}+\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{out}}\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{res}}-\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{in}}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{res}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{res}}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{res}}+

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}^{+}\;(d_{M}^{-}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{\mathrm{res}}+d_{M}\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{res}})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}^{-}d_{M}\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{+}\Bigg).

6.2.
Parallel ghost polarization

We can also choose again the “parallel ghost” polarization discussed in Section
5.1
. To be more explicit, and in preparation for the next section, let us fix
k=3
. Then we have
\mathfrak{g}=\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathbb{C})
where
M
is a 6-dimensional manifold with a complex structure, and
X=\mathfrak{g}[3]
.
58
58

58

We use freely the notation from Section
5.1
.
For later purposes, let us suppose that
M
is endowed with a Kähler metric
g
. We use the complex structure to define the polarization of the
\mathrm{gh}=0
component of
X
:

X^{[0]}=\Omega^{3}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)=\underbrace{\Omega^{3,0}(M)\oplus\Omega^{2,1}(M)}_{X^{+}}\oplus\underbrace{\Omega^{1,2}(M)\oplus\Omega^{0,3}(M)}_{X^{-}}.

Correspondingly, we split the field
\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{A}^{\leq 2}+\mathsf{A}^{3,+}+\mathsf{A}^{3,-}+\mathsf{A}^{\geq 4}
and similarly for
\mathsf{A}_{I}
. We denote by
\mathcal{P}^{[<0],-}
the polarization given by

\mathcal{P}^{[<0],-}=\left\{\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathsf{A}^{\geq 4}},\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathsf{A}^{3,-}}\right\},

whose base is parametrized by
(\mathsf{A}^{\leq 2},\mathsf{A}^{3,+})
, and by
\mathcal{P}^{[<0],+}
the similar polarization with
-
and
+
exchanged. The maps
d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+},d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}\colon X^{[1]}\to X^{[0]}
defined in Section
5.2
are given by projecting the de Rham differential

d_{M}\colon\Omega^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)\to\Omega^{3}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)

to
X^{\pm}
. Explicitly, they are given by

\displaystyle d^{+}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big|_{\Omega^{2,0}}

\displaystyle=d_{M}\big|_{\Omega^{2,0}},\qquad

\displaystyle d^{+}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big|_{\Omega^{1,1}}=\partial_{M}\big|_{\Omega^{1,1}},\qquad

\displaystyle d^{+}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big|_{\Omega^{0,2}}=0,

\displaystyle d^{-}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big|_{\Omega^{2,0}}

\displaystyle=0,\qquad

\displaystyle d^{-}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big|_{\Omega^{1,1}}=\bar{\partial}_{M}\big|_{\Omega^{1,1}},\qquad

\displaystyle d^{-}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big|_{\Omega^{0,2}}=d_{M}\big|_{\Omega^{0,2}},

see Figure
11
.
Now, we consider the cylinder
I\times M
with with
\mathcal{P}^{[<0],+}
on the in-boundary and
\mathcal{P}^{[<0],-}
on the out-boundary.
We then have the following fields in the effective action (referring to notation from Section
5.1
):

•

\psi_{\mathrm{out}}^{+}=\mathsf{A}^{3,+}_{\mathrm{out}}=\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{\mathrm{out}}+\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{out}}
— physical boundary field on out-boundary,

•

\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{in}}=\mathsf{A}^{3,-}_{\mathrm{in}}=\mathsf{A}^{0,3}_{\mathrm{in}}+\mathsf{A}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{in}}
— physical boundary field on in-boundary,

•

(\psi^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{in}},\psi^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{out}})=(\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\leq 2},\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{out}}^{\leq 2})
— boundary fields in higher ghost number (collected in a superfield),

•

A^{[1]}_{\mathrm{res}}=\mathsf{A}^{2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}=\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}^{2,0}+\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}^{1,1}+\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}^{0,2}
— 2-form, residual field in ghost number 0,

•

(A^{[>1]}_{\mathrm{res}},\psi_{\mathrm{res}})=(\mathsf{A}^{<2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{>3})
— residual fields of higher ghost number (form degree
<2
) or negative ghost number (form degree
>3
).

The effective action (
46
) then reads

S_{\mathrm{eff}}[\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{out}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{res}}]=S_{\mathrm{ph}}+S_{\mathrm{gh}},

where

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{ph}}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{\mathrm{out}}\mathsf{A}^{0,3}_{\mathrm{in}}+\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{out}}\mathsf{A}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{in}}+\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{\mathrm{out}}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}^{0,2}+\mathsf{A}^{0,3}_{\mathrm{in}}\partial\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}^{2,0}

\displaystyle+\int_{M}\mathsf{A}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{in}}\partial\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}^{1,1}+\mathsf{A}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{in}}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}^{2,0}+\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{out}}\partial\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}^{0,2}+\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{out}}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}^{1,1}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}^{1,1}\partial\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}^{1,1},

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{gh}}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}(\mathsf{A}^{\leq 2}_{\mathrm{out}}-\mathsf{A}^{\leq 2}_{\mathrm{in}})\mathsf{A}^{>3}_{\mathrm{res}}+\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{>3}d_{M}\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}^{<2}.

A similar formula holds for higher-dimensional Chern–Simons theories. Some comments:

i)

As a consequence of Propositions
5.4
and
5.3
, the effective action satisfies the modified quantum master equation, and the
\mathrm{gh}=0
part
S_{\mathrm{ph}}
satisfies the generalized Hamilton–Jacobi equations. In particular,
S_{\mathrm{ph}}
can be identified with the HJ action.

ii)

One can rewrite
S_{\mathrm{ph}}
as

S_{\mathrm{ph}}=\int_{M}(\mathsf{A}^{3,+}_{\mathrm{out}}-d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}\mathsf{A}^{2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}})(A^{3,-}_{\mathrm{in}}+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}^{2})-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\partial}A^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\partial A^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}

— a higher-dimensional version of an abelian gauged WZW model, see also footnote
22
.

6.2.1.
Pushforward over residual fields

The space of residual fields is

\mathcal{V}=\{dt\cdot\mathsf{A}^{\leq 2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}+\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{\geq 4}\}\;\;=\Omega^{\leq 2}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)[2]\oplus\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{\geq 4}(M)[3].

In particular, the components and their ghost numbers are

field

\mathsf{A}^{0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}

\mathsf{A}^{1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}

\mathsf{A}^{2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}

\mathsf{A}^{4}_{\mathrm{res}}

\mathsf{A}^{5}_{\mathrm{res}}

\mathsf{A}^{6}_{\mathrm{res}}

ghost number

2

1

0

-1

-2

-3

A gauge-fixing lagrangian can be constructed by using the Hodge decomposition for
\bar{\partial}
: Namely, using the Kähler metric
g
, we decompose

\Omega^{p,q}(M)=H^{p,q}(M)\oplus\Omega^{p,q}_{\bar{\partial}-\mathrm{ex}}\oplus\Omega^{p,q}_{\bar{\partial}-\mathrm{coex}}

where the middle and rightmost terms denote the spaces of
\bar{\partial}
-exact and
\bar{\partial}^{*}
-exact forms, respectively. The gauge-fixing lagrangian
\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{V}
is then defined as

(108)

\mathcal{L}=\bigoplus_{p+q\leq 2}\Omega^{p,q}_{\bar{\partial}-\mathrm{coex}}\oplus\bigoplus_{p+q\geq 4}\left(H^{p,q}(M)\oplus\Omega^{p,q}_{\bar{\partial}-\mathrm{coex}}\right).

Restricted to this gauge-fixing lagrangian, the effective action is nondegenerate in residual fields and the integral gives

Z_{*}\propto\delta(\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{\mathrm{out}}+\partial\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{out},\bar{\partial}\mathrm{-ex}})\,\delta(\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{in}}+\partial\mathsf{A}^{0,3}_{\mathrm{in},\bar{\partial}\mathrm{-ex}})\,\delta(\mathsf{A}^{\leq 2}_{\mathrm{out},\mathrm{harm}}-\mathsf{A}^{\leq 2}_{\mathrm{in},\mathrm{harm}})\cdot\\
\cdot\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\bigg(\int_{M}\big(\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{\mathrm{out}}\mathsf{A}^{0,3}_{\mathrm{in}}+\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{out}}(\mathrm{id}-P_{\bar{\partial}\mathrm{-ex}})\mathsf{A}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{in}}\big)-\\
-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M\times M}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{out}}(x)K(x,x^{\prime})\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{out}}(x^{\prime})-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M\times M}\partial\mathsf{A}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{in}}(x)K(x,x^{\prime})\partial\mathsf{A}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{in}}(x^{\prime})\bigg),

where
K(x,x^{\prime})
is the integral kernel of the inverse of the operator
(\partial\bar{\partial})
restricted to
\Omega^{1,1}_{\bar{\partial}-\mathrm{coex}}
.

6.3.
7D Chern–Simons and Kodaira–Spencer action functional

We now turn our attention to 7-dimensional Chern–Simons theory on a cylinder with a particular polarization on the out-boundary. This polarization was first discovered by Hitchin
[
29
]
. It was used in
[
26
]
to argue that the semi-classical approximation of the Chern–Simons wave function can be expressed in terms of the Kodaira–Spencer action functional introduced in
[
9
]
whose classical solutions are deformations of complex structures on a Kähler manifold (see Appendix
B
for a brief review of the Kodaira-Spencer theory). Here we argue that this semiclassical approximation is in fact exact in the axial gauge. From the general arguments of the BV-BFV formalism, it follows that a change of gauge fixing will result in an
\Omega
-exact change of the partition function, hence its
\Omega
-cohomology class is well-defined and given by the Kodaira–Spencer partition function. A caveat is that in this section we do not take care of determinants arising in Gaussian path integrals. Those might lead to anomalies similar to the discussion of Remark
4.4
, and would have to be treated separately.

6.3.1.
General polarizations in
4l+3
-dimensional Chern–Simons theory

Using the results of Section
5.2
, one can consider also more general polarizations in higher-dimensional Chern–Simons theories, in dimension
d=2k+1=4l+3
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{P}^{[0]}
is any polarization on
X^{[0]}=\Omega^{k}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
such that we have local coordinates
\mathsf{A}^{Q}
on the base and
\mathsf{A}^{P}
on the fibers, and let
G=G(\mathsf{A}^{-},\mathsf{A}^{Q})
be the corresponding generating function. From Section
5.2
we know that the partition function of abelian Chern–Simons theory with
\mathcal{P}^{[<0],+}
-polarization on the in-boundary and
\mathcal{P}^{[<0],P}
-polarization on the out-boundary is
Z=\exp(\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{\mathrm{eff}})
with
S_{\mathrm{eff}}=S_{\mathrm{ph}}+S_{\mathrm{gh}}
and

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{ph}}[\mathsf{A}^{-}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{Q}_{\mathrm{out}},\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\partial_{M}\mathsf{A}^{l,l}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\bar{\partial}_{M}\mathsf{A}^{l,l}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}+\int_{M}\mathsf{A}^{k,-}_{\mathrm{in}}d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}\mathsf{A}^{k-1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}

\displaystyle\qquad-G(\mathsf{A}^{k,-}_{\mathrm{in}}+d_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-}\mathsf{A}^{k-1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}},\mathsf{A}^{Q}_{\mathrm{out}}),

(109)

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{gh}}[\mathsf{A}^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{out}},\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}},\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{res}}]

\displaystyle=\int_{M}(\mathsf{A}^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{out}}-\mathsf{A}^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{in}})\mathsf{A}^{[<0]}_{\mathrm{res}}+\int_{M}\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{[<0]}d_{M}\mathsf{A}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}^{[>0]}.

See also the toy model in considered
[
16
, Section 12]
.

6.3.2.
Hitchin polarization on 6-dimensional manifolds and effective action

In 7-dimensional Chern–Simons theory, there is an interesting — nonlinear — polarization on the boundary phase space, coming from the special geometry of three-forms in six dimensions first described by Hitchin in
[
29
]
.
The idea is as follows. A complex 3-form
A
on a six-dimensional manifold with a complex structure decomposes as

(110)

A=A^{+,\mathrm{nl}}+A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}

where
A^{+,\mathrm{nl}}
and
A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}
are decomposable complex 3-forms, i.e., triple wedge products:

A^{\pm,\mathrm{nl}}=\theta_{1}^{\pm}\wedge\theta_{2}^{\pm}\wedge\theta_{3}^{\pm},\qquad\theta_{i}^{\pm}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}).

The
3
-form
A
is called
nondegenerate
if
A^{+,\mathrm{nl}}\wedge A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}
is everywhere nonvanishing (which is equivalent to the fact that the form
A
is not decomposable). In this case
A^{+,\mathrm{nl}}
and
A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}
are uniquely determined by
A
and define a polarization of
\Omega^{3}(M)_{\mathrm{nd}}
, the subset of nondegenerate forms. For more details on this polarization, we refer to
[
26
]
.
The effective action on the cylinder with
\mathcal{P}^{[<0],-,\mathrm{l}}
-polarization on the in-boundary
59
59

59

For consistency with
[
26
]
and
[
9
]
, we switch here to the polarization with base parametrized by
\mathsf{A}^{3,+,\mathrm{l}}
, as opposed to the rest of the paper.
and
\mathcal{P}^{[<0],+,\mathrm{nl}}
-polarization on the out-boundary thus reads
S_{\mathrm{eff}}=S_{\mathrm{ph}}+S_{\mathrm{gh}}
with
S_{\mathrm{gh}}
given by (
109
) and

(111)

S_{\mathrm{ph}}[\mathsf{A}^{+,\mathrm{l}}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{-,\mathrm{nl}}_{\mathrm{out}},\mathsf{A}^{2,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}},\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}},\mathsf{A}^{0,2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}]\\
=\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\partial\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}+\int_{M}\mathsf{A}^{+,\mathrm{l}}_{\mathrm{in}}d\mathsf{A}^{0,2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}+\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{in}}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}-G(\mathsf{A}^{+,\l}_{\mathrm{in}}+d\mathsf{A}^{2,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}+\partial\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}};\mathsf{A}^{-,\mathrm{nl}}_{\mathrm{out}}).

Proposition 6.1
.

The partition function is given by

Z=\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\left(S_{\mathrm{ph}}+S_{\mathrm{gh}}\right),

with
S_{\mathrm{ph}}
given by (
111
) and
S_{\mathrm{gh}}
given by (
109
). It satisfies the modified quantum master equation

(\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}+\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathrm{res}})Z=0,

with
\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}
,
\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}
given by the standard quantization of the BFV boundary action
\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\mathsf{A}d\mathsf{A}
:

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}d\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{in}}^{2}\left(\mathsf{A}^{+,\mathrm{l}}_{\mathrm{in}}-i\hbar\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{in}}^{+,\mathrm{l}}}\right)-i\hbar\int_{M}d\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{in}}^{1}\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{in}}^{4}}-i\hbar\int_{M}d\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{in}}^{0}\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{in}}^{5}},

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}d\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{out}}^{2}\left(\mathsf{A}^{-,\mathrm{nl}}_{\mathrm{out}}-i\hbar\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathsf{A}^{-,\mathrm{nl}}_{\mathrm{out}}}\right)-i\hbar\int_{M}d\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{out}}^{1}\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{out}}^{4}}-i\hbar\int_{M}d\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{out}}^{0}\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{out}}^{5}}.

Proof.

We are in the situation of Remark
5.8
here because of the fact that the boundary action is linear in the canonical variables defining the Hitchin polarization, see equation (
110
). It follows that the mQME is satisfied for the standard quantization of the boundary action.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

We want to stress that in this gauge, the semiclassical approximation to this partition function that was used as an ansatz in
[
26
]
(the integral kernel of the generalized Segal--Bargmann transform
60
60

60

See Appendix
A
for a motivation for the comparison of BV-BFV partition function with standard Segal–Bargmann transform.
from the linear to the nonlinear polarization) is found to be
exact
: there are no quantum corrections. Adapting the proof of
[
16
, Appendix B]
to the infinite-dimensional setting one can show that changing the gauge fixing by changing the propagator on the interval
results in a change of the partition function by a
(\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathrm{res}}-\Omega)
-cocycle.

Remark 6.3
.

To be precise in comparison with
[
26
]
, one should identify the residual fields in (
111
) with the Lagrange multipliers enforcing the constraint
d_{M}\mathsf{A}=0
in
[
26
]
. This, of course, is precisely their role when interpreting (
111
) as the Hamilton–Jacobi action for 7D Chern–Simons theory in the chosen polarizations.

6.3.3.
Comparison with Kodaira–Spencer gravity

Following
[
26
]
, we want to compare the Chern–Simons effective action (
111
) with the Kodaira–Spencer action functional (
145
).
Let us fix a reference holomorphic 3-form
\omega_{0}\in\Omega^{3,0}(M)
. Its conjugate is an antiholomorphic 3-form
\overline{\omega}_{0}\in\Omega^{0,3}(M)
. We can then parametrize
A^{+,\mathrm{nl}}
and
A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}
as

\displaystyle A^{+,\mathrm{nl}}

\displaystyle=\rho e^{\mu}\omega_{0},

\displaystyle A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}

\displaystyle=\overline{\rho}e^{\overline{\mu}}\overline{\omega}_{0},

where
\rho,\overline{\rho}\in\Omega^{0}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
,
\mu\in\Omega^{-1,1}(M)
,
\overline{\mu}\in\Omega^{1,-1}(M)
and

(112)

\rho e^{\mu}\omega_{0}=\rho\left(\omega_{0}+\mu\omega_{0}+\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}\omega_{0}+\frac{\mu^{3}}{6}\omega_{0}\right),

where
\mu\omega_{0}
should be interpreted as extension of contraction to forms with values in vector fields.

Of course, a complex 3-form still has a decomposition
A=A^{+,\mathrm{l}}+A^{-,\mathrm{l}}
, with
A^{+,\mathrm{l}}\in\Omega^{3,0}(M)\oplus\Omega^{2,1}(M)
, and
A^{-,\mathrm{l}}\in\Omega^{1,2}(M)\oplus\Omega^{0,3}(M)
.

The following expression for
G
is given in
[
26
]
:

(113)

G(A^{3,0},A^{2,1},\overline{\rho},\overline{\mu})=\\
=\int_{M}\overline{\rho}(A^{3,0}\overline{\omega}_{0}+A^{2,1}\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0})+\overline{\rho}^{2}\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}-\frac{\left\langle\left((A^{2,1}-\frac{1}{2}\overline{\rho}\,\overline{\mu}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0})^{\vee}\right)^{3}\right\rangle}{(A^{3,0})^{\vee}-\overline{\rho}\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle}\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}.

For completeness, we present a derivation of this formula in Appendix
B.2
.
Here for
\overline{\mu}\in\Omega^{1,-1}(M)
the function
\langle\mu^{3}\rangle
is defined in (
127
) and for any
A\in\Omega^{p,q}
we
define
A^{\vee}\in\Omega^{p-3,q}
by
A^{\vee}\omega_{0}=A
.

Consider now the state

(114)

\psi\left(\overline{\rho}_{\mathrm{out}},\overline{\mu}_{\mathrm{out}},\overline{\mathsf{A}^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{out}}}\right)=\delta(\overline{\mu}_{\mathrm{out}})\delta\left(\mathsf{A}^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{out}}\right)\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\int_{M}\overline{\rho}_{\mathrm{out}}\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}.

This is an extension of the physical state
61
61

61

This state
\psi_{\mathrm{ph}}
was proposed in
[
26
]
as a way of “fixing the string coupling constant.” It is a quantization of the lagrangian given by
\overline{\mu}=0
,
p_{\overline{\rho}}=1
, where
p_{\overline{\rho}}
denotes the canonical momentum of
\overline{\rho}
.

\psi_{\mathrm{ph}}(\overline{\mu},\overline{\rho})=\delta(\overline{\mu}_{\mathrm{out}})\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\int_{M}\overline{\rho}_{\mathrm{out}}\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}

to the ghost sector. Note that this state trivially satisfies the mQME because
\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}\psi_{\mathrm{ph}}
is linear in the ghost fields, but we are multiplying with the ghost delta function.
62
62

62

This is just the identity
x\delta(x)=0
.

Now we compute (formally) the vector
Z\left|\psi\right\rangle
(put differently, we are specifying a boundary condition on the
\mathrm{out}
-boundary for the quantum theory).
This means computing the following functional integral:

\displaystyle Z\left|\psi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}\overline{\rho}_{\mathrm{out}}\mathcal{D}\overline{\mu}_{\mathrm{out}}\mathcal{D}\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{out}}^{[>0]}Z\cdot\psi

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}\overline{\rho}_{\mathrm{out}}Z[\mathsf{A}^{+,\mathrm{l}}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{in}},\overline{\rho}_{\mathrm{out}},0,0,\mathsf{A}^{\geq 4}_{\mathrm{res}},\mathsf{A}^{\leq 2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}]\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\int_{M}\overline{\rho}_{\mathrm{out}}\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z^{\prime}[\mathsf{A}^{+,\mathrm{l}}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{\geq 4}_{\mathrm{res}},\mathsf{A}^{\leq 2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}].

The partition function
Z
depends on
\overline{\rho}_{\mathrm{out}}
only through
G
and we have

(115)

\int\mathcal{D}\overline{\rho}\;e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}G(A^{3,0},A^{2,1},\overline{\rho},0)+\frac{i}{\hbar}\int_{M}\overline{\rho}\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}}=\\
=\delta(A^{3,0}-\omega_{0})\,\exp\left(-\frac{i}{\hbar}\int_{M}\frac{1}{6}\langle A^{2,1},A^{2,1},A^{2,1}\rangle\right).

Here for
A\in\Omega^{2,1}(M)
we have
\frac{1}{6}\langle A,A,A\rangle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\langle(A^{\vee})^{3}\rangle\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}
. Thus,
Z^{\prime}
has the following expression:

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}

\displaystyle=Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{ph}}Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{gh}},\quad\mbox{where}

(116)

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{ph}}

\displaystyle=\delta(\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{\mathrm{in}}+\partial\mathsf{A}^{2,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}-\omega_{0})\,\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\partial\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}+\int_{M}\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{\mathrm{in}}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{0,2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}

\displaystyle+\int_{M}\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{in}}\partial\mathsf{A}^{0,2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}+\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{in}}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}-\langle((\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{in}}^{2,1}+\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{2,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}+\partial\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}})^{\vee})^{3}\rangle\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}\bigg),

(117)

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{gh}}

\displaystyle=\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\left(\int_{M}-\mathsf{A}^{\leq 2}_{\mathrm{in}}\mathsf{A}^{\geq 4}_{\mathrm{res}}+\int_{M}\mathsf{A}^{\geq 4}_{\mathrm{res}}d_{M}\mathsf{A}^{\leq 1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\right).

We stress that
Z^{\prime}
was obtained from
Z
through a formal functional integral.
However, we have the following result.

Lemma 6.4
.

The function
Z^{\prime}[\mathsf{A}^{+,\mathrm{l}}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{\geq 4}_{\mathrm{res}},\mathsf{A}^{\leq 2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}]
satisfies the modified quantum master equation, i.e., is an
(\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathrm{res}})
-cocycle, where
\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}=\Omega^{(0)}_{\mathrm{in}}+\Omega^{(1)}_{\mathrm{in}}
is the standard quantization of
-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\mathsf{A}d_{M}\mathsf{A}
in the
\mathcal{P}^{[<0],-}
-polarization, explicitly given by

\displaystyle\Omega^{(0)}_{\mathrm{in}}

\displaystyle=-\int_{M}\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{in}}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{\mathrm{in}}+\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{in}}\partial\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{in}}^{0,2}+\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{in}}^{3,0}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{in}}^{0,2},

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\hbar}\Omega^{(1)}_{\mathrm{in}}

\displaystyle=-\int_{M}\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{\mathrm{in}}}\partial\mathsf{A}^{2,0}_{\mathrm{in}}+\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{in}}}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{2,0}_{\mathrm{in}}+\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{in}}}\partial\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{\mathrm{in}}+\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathsf{A}^{\leq 2}_{\mathrm{in}}}d\mathsf{A}^{\leq 1}_{\mathrm{in}}.

This is expected because
\psi
satisfies
\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}\psi=0
and
Z
satisfies the mQME. See also the discussions of gluing in
[
16
, Sections 11.4,12.2]
.
The interpretation of this Lemma is that
Z^{\prime}
is a valid state in the linear polarization.

Proof.

We will only prove the claim in ghost number 0, since in positive ghost number the effective action is the same as for linear polarizations. To begin, we note that any function of
\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{\mathrm{in}}+\partial\mathsf{A}^{2,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}
or
\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{in}}^{2,1}+\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{2,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}+\partial\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}
is
(i\hbar\{S_{\mathrm{gh}},\bullet\}_{\mathrm{res}}-\Omega^{(1)}_{\mathrm{in}})
-closed since
\{S_{\mathrm{gh}},\bullet\}_{\mathrm{res}}\big|_{\mathrm{gh}=0}=\int_{M}\mathsf{A}^{i,j}_{\mathrm{in}}\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathsf{A}^{i,j}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}}
.
This implies that the delta function in (
116
) and the last term in (
117
) are
\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathrm{res}}-\Omega^{(1)}
closed. It is a straightforward check that the remaining exponential terms are
(\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathrm{res}}-\Omega^{(0)}-\Omega^{(1)})
-closed, which concludes the proof. ∎

We will now argue that formally integrating out the residual fields, in ghost number 0 we obtain the Kodaira–Spencer action. Let us restrict to the gauge-fixing lagrangian
\mathcal{L}
defined similarly to (
108
), but given in ghost number 0 by
\partial^{*}
-exact 2-forms. We will denote

Z^{\prime\prime}[\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{in}}]=\int_{\mathcal{L}}Z^{\prime}[\mathsf{A}^{+,\mathrm{l}}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{\geq 4}_{\mathrm{res}},\mathsf{A}^{\leq 2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}].

The modified quantum master equation implies that for a
(2,0)
-form
\chi
one has

Z^{\prime}[\mathsf{A}^{+,\mathrm{l}}_{\mathrm{in}}+d\chi,\mathsf{A}^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{\geq 4}_{\mathrm{res}},\mathsf{A}^{\leq 2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}]=Z^{\prime}[\mathsf{A}^{+,\mathrm{l}}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{\geq 4}_{\mathrm{res}},\mathsf{A}^{\leq 2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}-\chi].

By a change of variables, this implies

Z^{\prime\prime}[\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{\mathrm{in}}+\partial\chi,\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{in}}+\bar{\partial}\chi,\mathsf{A}^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{in}}]=Z^{\prime\prime}[\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathsf{A}^{[>0]}_{\mathrm{in}}].

We can use this property to reduce the computation of
Z^{\prime\prime}
to the case

\mathsf{A}^{3,0}=\rho_{0}\omega_{0}
, where
\rho_{0}
is a constant. The
\delta
function in
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{ph}}
then factorizes as
\delta(\rho_{0}-1)\delta(\partial A^{2,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}})
. Since
\partial
is an isomorphism on the gauge-fixing lagrangian, the integral over
\mathsf{A}^{2,0}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}
gives

Z^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{ph}}=\int\mathcal{D}A^{0,2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}A^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\delta(\rho_{0}-1)\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\bigg(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\partial\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}+\\
+\int_{M}\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{in}}\partial\mathsf{A}^{0,2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}+\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{in}}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}-\langle((\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{in}}^{2,1}+\partial\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}})^{\vee})^{3}\rangle\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}\bigg)

and the integral over
A^{0,2}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}
then gives

Z^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{ph}}=\int\mathcal{D}\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\delta(\rho_{0}-1)\delta(\partial\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{in}}^{2,1})\cdot\\
\cdot\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\bigg(\int_{M}\frac{1}{2}\partial\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}+\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{in}}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}-\langle((\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{in}}^{2,1}+\partial\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}})^{\vee})^{3}\rangle\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}\bigg).

Finally, writing
\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{in}}
in the Hodge decomposition
\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{\mathrm{in}}=x+\partial\lambda+\partial^{*}\tau
, we obtain by another change of variables
63
63

63

We can choose
\lambda
such that
\partial^{*}\lambda=0
, so that
b\in\mathcal{L}\cap\Omega^{1,1}(M)
.

b=\mathsf{A}^{1,1}_{I\,\mathrm{res}}+\lambda
the expression

(118)

Z^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{ph}}[\rho_{0},x,\lambda,\tau]=\delta(\rho_{0}-1)\delta(\partial\partial^{*}\tau)\cdot\\
\cdot\int_{\mathcal{L}\cap\Omega^{1,1}(M)}\mathcal{D}b\;\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\int_{M}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\partial\lambda\bar{\partial}\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\partial b\bar{\partial}b+\bar{\partial}b\partial\lambda+\frac{1}{6}\langle(x+\partial b),(x+\partial b),(x+\partial b)\rangle\right),

which coincides with eq. (2.50) in
[
26
]
.
Thus, we see that the Chern–Simons partition function on a cylinder, paired with the state (
114
), coincides with the partition function of Kodaira–Spencer theory with background
x
and action functional given in (
145
) for
\lambda=0
. The latter integral can be evaluated perturbatively in terms of Feynman graphs and rules. It would be interesting to compare our results to other constructions of the BCOV theory, such as in
[
17
]
.

Remark 6.5

(On gauge invariance of
Z^{\prime\prime}
)
.

If one uses formally the properties of the BV integral, it is immediate that the
Z^{\prime\prime}
gives a class in
\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}
-cohomology independent of the gauge-fixing lagrangian
\mathcal{L}
.
64
64

64

To give a rigorous proof of this statement would require to give a strict interpretation of the functional integral in (
118
), which is beyond the scope of the present paper. It should be noted that the restriction of
b
to the subspace
\partial^{*}b=0
is a gauge-fixing condition for the KS theory, so one should consider also the the gauge independence of
Z_{KS}
.
We see here that this cohomology class has a representative given in terms of the KS partition function. The partition function
Z^{\prime\prime}
can be also interpreted as the BV-BFV partition function on the cylinder paired with the state
\psi
at the
\mathrm{out}
-boundary, with
all
fields integrated out using an axial-type gauge (the components of the gauge field involving
dt
are set to zero). Another open question is how
Z^{\prime\prime}
behaves when we deform away from this type of gauge to a general gauge fixing on the cylinder (say, one given by a Riemannian metric). This is a subject of ongoing research.

Appendix A
Segal–Bargmann transform via BV-BFV

Recall (see
[
28
]
for details) that the Segal–Bargmann space
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{SB}}
is the Hilbert space of holomorphic functions
\psi(z)
on
\mathbb{C}
satisfying

\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{i}{4\pi\hbar}dz\,d\bar{z}\;e^{-\frac{|z|^{2}}{2\hbar}}\;\overline{\psi(z)}\psi(z)<\infty

(here we assume that
\hbar
is a fixed positive number), equipped with inner product

(119)

\langle\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{i}{4\pi\hbar}dz\,d\bar{z}\;e^{-\frac{|z|^{2}}{2\hbar}}\;\overline{\psi_{1}(z)}\psi_{2}(z).

The Segal–Bargmann space is isomorphic to the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
of square-integrable functions on
\mathbb{R}
, with the unitary isomorphism
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{SB}}
given by the Segal–Bargmann transform:

(120)

\chi(x)\mapsto\psi(z)=(\pi\hbar)^{-\frac{1}{4}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}dx\;e^{-\frac{1}{\hbar}\left(\frac{z^{2}}{4}-zx+\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)}\;\chi(x).

Now we would like to show how the transformation (
120
) can be seen as the partition function for topological quantum mechanics on an interval with appropriate boundary polarizations.

Consider topological quantum mechanics on the interval
I
parametrized by
0\leq t\leq 1
— the theory with
0
-form fields
x,p\in\Omega^{0}(I)
and action

(121)

S=\int_{I}p\,dx.

In the BV-BFV formalism, we adjoin the anti-fields
x^{*},p^{*}\in\Omega^{1}(I)
—
1
-form fields carrying ghost number
-1
(while
x,p
carry ghost number
0
), so that the odd symplectic form on BV fields is:
\int_{I}\delta x\wedge\delta x^{*}+\delta p\wedge\delta p^{*}
. The BFV phase space assigned to a point
\mathrm{pt}^{\pm}
(where
\pm
is the orientation) is:
\mathcal{F}^{\partial}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\;\ni(x,p)
with the Noether
1
-form
\alpha_{\mathrm{pt}^{\pm}}=\pm p\,\delta x
and vanishing BFV action
S_{\mathrm{pt}}=0
.

Alongside the real coordinates
x,p
on the phase space, we will consider the complex coordinates
z=x-ip
,
\bar{z}=x+ip
. The symplectic structure on the phase space is
\omega_{\mathrm{pt}^{\pm}}=\delta\alpha_{\mathrm{pt}^{\pm}}=\pm\delta p\wedge\delta x
. Written in complex coordinates it has the form
\mp\frac{i}{2}\delta\bar{z}\wedge\delta z
.

Consider the polarization
\mathrm{Span}\{\frac{\partial}{\partial p}\}
(i.e.,
x
fixed) at
t=0
and the polarization
\mathrm{Span}\{\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}\}
(i.e.,
z
fixed) at
t=1
. The corresponding modification of the action (
121
) by a boundary term is:

(122)

S^{f}=S\underbrace{-\big(\frac{i}{4}x^{2}+\frac{1}{2}xp+\frac{i}{4}p^{2}\big)}_{f}\Big|_{t=1}

— this boundary term is chosen so that one has
-\frac{i}{2}\bar{z}\delta z=p\delta x+\delta f
. Thus, the corresponding boundary Noether
1
-form is:

(123)

\alpha_{\partial I}^{f}=-\frac{i}{2}\bar{z}\,\delta z\big|_{t=1}-p\,\delta x\big|_{t=0}

— it vanishes along the chosen polarization, as desired.

Next consider the following splitting of the (complexified) phase space

\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathfrak{g}^{+}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{-},

where
\mathfrak{g}^{+}
is parametrized by
z
and
\mathfrak{g}^{-}
is parametrized by
x
(we are borrowing the notations from (
5
) here). The space of fields
\mathcal{F}=\Omega^{\bullet}(I,\mathfrak{g}^{+})\oplus\Omega^{\bullet}(I,\mathfrak{g}^{-})
is fibered over
\mathcal{B}\ni(z_{\mathrm{out}},x_{\mathrm{in}})
with the fiber

\mathcal{Y}=\Omega^{\bullet}(I,\{1\};\mathfrak{g}^{+})\oplus\Omega^{\bullet}(I,\{0\};\mathfrak{g}^{-})=\\
=\underbrace{\big(\Omega^{0}(I,\{1\};\mathfrak{g}^{+})\oplus\Omega^{0}(I,\{0\};\mathfrak{g}^{-})\big)}_{\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{K-ex}}\bigoplus\underbrace{\big(\Omega^{1}(I;\mathfrak{g}^{+}[-1])\oplus\Omega^{1}(I;\mathfrak{g}^{-}[-1])\big)}_{\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{d-ex}}.

This is an acyclic complex, and thus we can choose the space of residual fields to be zero (cf. Remark
3.1
). The corresponding propagator — the integral kernel of the chain contraction
K
— is:

\eta(t,t^{\prime})=-\pi^{+}\otimes\theta(t^{\prime}-t)+\pi^{-}\otimes\theta(t-t^{\prime}).

The BV-BFV partition function is then given by the following path integral

(124)

Z(z_{\mathrm{out}},x_{\mathrm{in}})=\int\mathcal{D}z_{\mathrm{fl}}\;\mathcal{D}x_{\mathrm{fl}}\;e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S^{f}(\widetilde{z_{\mathrm{out}}}+z_{\mathrm{fl}},\widetilde{x_{\mathrm{in}}}+x_{\mathrm{fl}})}\\
=\int\mathcal{D}z_{\mathrm{fl}}\;\mathcal{D}x_{\mathrm{fl}}\;e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\Big(i\int_{I}z_{\mathrm{fl}}\,dx_{\mathrm{fl}}+\big(-ix_{\mathrm{fl}}(1)z_{\mathrm{out}}+\frac{i}{4}z^{2}_{\mathrm{out}}\big)+\big(-iz_{\mathrm{fl}}(0)\,x_{\mathrm{in}}+\frac{i}{2}x_{\mathrm{in}}^{2}\big)\Big)}\\
=e^{-\frac{1}{\hbar}\big(\frac{z^{2}_{\mathrm{out}}}{4}-z_{\mathrm{out}}x_{\mathrm{in}}+\frac{x^{2}_{\mathrm{in}}}{2}\big)}.

Here we have a contribution from the Feynman diagram with single propagator connecting
z_{\mathrm{out}}
and
x_{\mathrm{in}}
. In (
124
) we recognize the integral kernel of the Segal–Bargmann transform (
120
). This is, of course, to be expected: the partition function for a cylinder (in this case, an interval), with polarization
\mathcal{P}_{1}
on the in-boundary and polarization
\mathcal{P}_{2}
on the out-boundary, maps
\mathcal{P}_{1}
-states to
\mathcal{P}_{2}
-states.

Remark A.1
.

Note that the Hamilton–Jacobi action for the theory (
121
) on the interval with our choice of in/out polarizations is:

S_{\text{HJ}}=S^{f}\big(x(t)=x_{\mathrm{in}},z(t)=z_{\mathrm{out}}\big)\quad=\quad\frac{i}{4}z_{\mathrm{out}}^{2}-iz_{\mathrm{out}}x_{\mathrm{in}}+\frac{i}{2}x_{\mathrm{in}}^{2}.

Here we again recognize the expression in the exponent in (
120
).

Finally, the measure
e^{-\frac{|z|^{2}}{2\hbar}}
in (
119
) from the BV-BFV standpoint originates from the gluing of intervals (more precisely, from gluing the out-end of one interval with
z
-fixed polarization to the in-end of another interval with
\bar{z}
-fixed polarization). Indeed, consider the theory (
121
) on the interval
I=[t_{0},t_{1}]
with some polarization
\mathcal{P}
at
t_{0}
and
z
-fixed polarization at
t_{1}
, and also the same theory on the interval
I^{\prime}=[t_{1},t_{2}]
, with some polarization
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
at
t_{2}
and with
\bar{z}
-fixed polarization at
t_{1}
.

The respective actions including the boundary terms adjusting for the polarization are

S^{f}_{I}=-\frac{i}{2}\int_{I}\bar{z}dz+f^{\mathcal{P}}\big|_{t_{0}}\quad,\quad S^{f}_{I^{\prime}}=-\frac{i}{2}\int_{I^{\prime}}\bar{z}dz-\frac{i}{2}z\bar{z}\big|_{t_{1}}+f^{\mathcal{P^{\prime}}}\big|_{t_{2}},

with
f^{\mathcal{P}},f^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
the appropriate
65
65

65

I.e., chosen in such a way that
\frac{i}{2}\bar{z}\delta z+\delta f^{\mathcal{P}}
vanishes along
\mathcal{P}
and
-\frac{i}{2}\bar{z}\delta z+\delta f^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
vanishes along
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.
boundary terms at
t_{0}
,
t_{2}
. It follows that the partition function for the glued interval
I\cup I^{\prime}=[t_{0},t_{2}]
is:

(125)

Z_{I\cup I^{\prime}}(v_{\mathrm{out}},u_{\mathrm{in}})=\int\mathcal{D}z(t)\,\mathcal{D}\bar{z}(t)\;e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}(S^{f}_{I}+\frac{i}{2}z\bar{z}\big|_{t_{1}}+S^{f}_{I^{\prime}})}=\\
=\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{i\,dz\,d\bar{z}}{4\pi\hbar}\;Z_{I^{\prime}}(v_{\mathrm{out}},\bar{z})\;e^{-\frac{z\bar{z}}{2\hbar}}\;Z_{I}(z,u_{\mathrm{in}}).

In the first integral, the term
\frac{i}{2}z\bar{z}\big|_{t_{1}}
compensates the boundary term of
S^{f}_{I^{\prime}}
at
t_{1}
. The final integral is over the values of
z,\bar{z}
at
t=t_{1}
. Also, we denoted
u_{\mathrm{in}}
a coordinate parametrizing the space of leaves of the polarization
\mathcal{P}
and similarly
v_{\mathrm{out}}
a coordinate paramterizing the space of leaves of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. In (
125
), we see the Segal–Bargmann measure
\frac{i\,dz\,d\bar{z}}{4\pi\hbar}\,e^{-\frac{|z|^{2}}{2\hbar}}
, cf. (
119
), appearing.
The normalization factor
\frac{i}{4\pi\hbar}
is chosen in such a way that, choosing
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\mathrm{Span}\{\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}\}
, we have

Z_{I\cup I^{\prime}}(z_{\mathrm{out}},u_{\mathrm{in}})=Z_{I}(z_{\mathrm{out}},u_{\mathrm{in}}),

which in turn follows from the identity

\int\frac{i}{4\pi\hbar}dz\,d\bar{z}\;\underbrace{e^{\frac{z_{\mathrm{out}}\bar{z}}{2\hbar}}}_{Z_{I^{\prime}}(z_{\mathrm{out}},\bar{z})}e^{-\frac{|z|^{2}}{2\hbar}}\Psi(z)=\Psi(z_{\mathrm{out}})

true for any holomorphic function
\Psi(z)
(for which the l.h.s. converges), applied to
\Psi(z)=Z_{I}(z,u_{\mathrm{in}})
.

A.1.
Aside: contour integration in the complexified space of fields, a lattice toy model

Throughout the paper we are dealing with complexified phase spaces (so that we can impose the convenient holomorphic/antiholomorphic polarizations) and complexified spaces of fields where the path integral should be understood as an integral over a real contour.

A toy model is provided by topological quantum mechanics
S=\int_{I}pdx
, as above. The phase space for a point is
\Phi=\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We consider the model with boundary polarization
\mathrm{Span}\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\right\}
(i.e.,
\bar{z}
fixed) at
t=0
and
\mathrm{Span}\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}\right\}
(i.e.,
z
fixed) at
t=1
; these polarizations are defined on the complexified phace space
\Phi_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
. The action modified by the appropriate boundary term is
S^{f}=\int_{I}(-\frac{i}{2}\bar{z}dz)-\frac{i}{2}z\bar{z}\big|_{t=0}
.

The path integral for this model can be presented by a lattice approximation (which happens to be exact):

(126)

Z(z_{\mathrm{out}}=z_{N},\bar{z}_{\mathrm{in}}=\bar{z}_{0})=\int_{C\subset Y_{\mathbb{C}}}\prod_{k=1}^{N-1}\frac{i\,dz_{k}d\bar{z}_{k}}{4\pi\hbar}\;\;e^{\frac{1}{2\hbar}\big(\sum_{k=1}^{N}(z_{k}-z_{k-1})\bar{z}_{k-1}+z_{0}\bar{z}_{0}\big)}\\
=\int_{C\subset Y_{\mathbb{C}}}\prod_{k=1}^{N-1}\frac{i\,dz_{k}d\bar{z}_{k}}{4\pi\hbar}\;\;e^{\frac{1}{2\hbar}\big(z_{1}\bar{z}_{0}-z_{1}\bar{z}_{1}+z_{2}\bar{z}_{1}-z_{2}\bar{z}_{2}+\cdots-z_{N-1}\bar{z}_{N-1}+z_{N}\bar{z}_{N-1}\big)}.

Here:

•

We understand that the interval
I=[0,1]
is partitioned into
N\geq 1
smaller intervals
[t_{0}=0,t_{1}]
,
[t_{1},t_{2}]
, …,
[t_{N-1},t_{N}=1]
.

•

The space
Y_{\mathbb{C}}=(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{N-1}
— the fiber of the complexified space of fields over boundary conditions — is the product of complexified phase spaces corresponding to
t_{1},\ldots,t_{N-1}
. In particular, we understand that
z_{k}
and
\bar{z}_{k}
are independent complex variables: they do not have to be complex conjugates of each other.

•

The integration is over a “contour”
C\subset Y_{\mathbb{C}}
— a real
2(N-1)
-dimensional subspace. In particular, the integrand in (
126
) is a holomorphic
2(N-1)
-form on
Y_{\mathbb{C}}
pulled back to
C
by the inclusion.

For the contour
C
, we can consider the following two examples:

(i)

Contour
C_{1}
given by
\bar{z}_{k}=z_{k}^{*}
,
k=1,\ldots,N-1
, where
*
is the complex conjugation.

(ii)

Contour
C_{2}
given by reality conditions
z_{k}\in\mathbb{R}\subset\mathbb{C}
,
\bar{z}_{k}\in i\mathbb{R}\subset\mathbb{C}
.

For
C=C_{1}
, (
126
) is an absolutely convergent Gaussian integral (for arbitrary boundary conditions) and yields

Z(z_{\mathrm{out}},\bar{z}_{\mathrm{in}})=e^{\frac{1}{2\hbar}\bar{z}_{\mathrm{in}}z_{\mathrm{out}}}.

For
C=C_{2}
, (
126
) is an oscillatory Fresnel integral which is only conditionally convergent and even that only under special assumptions on the boundary conditions (
\bar{z}_{\mathrm{in}}\in i\mathbb{R},z_{\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{R}
).
66
66

66

Absolute/conditional convergence property is particularly clear in the simplest case
N=2
: here (
126
) becomes
\int_{C\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}}\frac{i\,dz_{1}d\bar{z}_{1}}{4\pi\hbar}\;e^{\frac{1}{2\hbar}(z_{1}\bar{z}_{0}-z_{1}\bar{z}_{1}+z_{2}\bar{z}_{1})}
.

When the integral over
C_{2}
converges, its value coincides with the result of integration over
C_{1}
(which is clear, e.g., from a contour deformation argument).

In summary, we have the complexified space of fields of the lattice theory
F^{I}_{\mathbb{C}}\ni(\bar{z}_{0},z_{1},\bar{z}_{1},\ldots,z_{N-1},\bar{z}_{N-1},z_{N})
fibered over the complex space of boundary conditions
\mathcal{B}^{\partial I}_{\mathbb{C}}\ni\{\bar{z}_{\mathrm{in}},z_{\mathrm{out}}\}
with complex fiber
Y_{\mathbb{C}}
(lattice fields with zero boundary conditions), and the integration in the lattice path integral (
126
) is over a contour
C\subset Y_{\mathbb{C}}
— a half-dimensional real submanifold.

Appendix B
Kodaira–Spencer theory

We briefly review the definition of the Kodaira–Spencer action functional that was introduced in
[
9
, Section 5]
, where it was used to analyze the target space physics of the
B
-model. See also
[
26
, Section 2.1]
.

B.1.
Some operations on complex forms

Let
M
be a 6-dimensional Calabi–Yau manifold with a reference holomorphic 3-form
\omega_{0}
(sometimes the pair
(M,\omega_{0})
is called a gauged Calabi–Yau manifold). We denote by
\Omega^{p,q}(M)
complex forms of Hodge type
(p,q)
— sections of the bundle
\wedge^{p}(T_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}M)^{1,0}\otimes\wedge^{q}(T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)^{0,1}
and by
\Omega^{-p,q}(M)
sections of the bundle
\wedge^{p}(T_{\mathbb{C}}M)^{1,0}\otimes\wedge^{q}(T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)^{0,1}
, i.e.,
(0,q)
-forms with values in
(p,0)
-vector fields. Contraction with the reference holomorphic 3-form provides a map

\displaystyle\Omega^{-p,q}(M)

\displaystyle\to\Omega^{3-p,q}(M)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapsto A^{\vee}=A\omega_{0}

(we omit the symbols for wedge products and contractions). For a
(p,q)
-form
A
with
p\geq 0
, we set
A^{\vee}=A\omega_{0}^{-1}\in\Omega^{p-3,q}
, in particular we have
(A^{\vee})^{\vee}=A
. For
A,B,C\in\Omega^{-1,1}(M)
, we further define the operations

\displaystyle A^{\vee}\circ B^{\vee}

\displaystyle=(AB)^{\vee}=(AB)\omega_{0}

\displaystyle\in\Omega^{1,2}(M),

\displaystyle\langle A^{\vee},B^{\vee},C^{\vee}\rangle

\displaystyle=A^{\vee}(B^{\vee}\circ C^{\vee})=A^{\vee}(BC)\omega_{0}

\displaystyle\in\Omega^{3,3}(M),

(127)

\displaystyle\langle A^{3}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}\frac{\langle A^{\vee},A^{\vee},A^{\vee}\rangle}{\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}}=\frac{1}{6}(A^{3}\omega_{0})(\overline{\omega}_{0})^{-1}

\displaystyle\in\Omega^{0,0}(M),

and the same operations make sense for
\bar{A},\bar{B},\bar{C}\in\Omega^{1,-1}(M)
if we replace
\omega_{0}
by
\bar{\omega}_{0}
. The minus sign in (
127
) ensures that
\langle A^{3}\rangle\overline{\omega}_{0}=\frac{1}{6}A^{3}\omega_{0}
. Also, in the last equation we made use of the fact that one can divide by sections of a line bundle.
By a lemma of Tian
[
40
]
, if
\partial A=\partial B=0
, we have

(128)

[A,B]^{\vee}=\partial(A^{\vee}\circ B^{\vee}).

B.2.
The generating function for Hitchin polarization

For completeness, we include here a derivation of the generating function (
113
) for the transformation from the linear polarization to the nonlinear polarization.
A complex 3-form
A
has decompositions
A=A^{+,\mathrm{l}}+A^{-,\mathrm{l}}
, with
A^{+,\mathrm{l}}\in\Omega^{3,0}(M)\oplus\Omega^{2,1}(M)
, and
A^{-,\mathrm{l}}\in\Omega^{1,2}(M)\oplus\Omega^{0,3}(M)
, and

A=A^{+,\mathrm{nl}}+A^{-,\mathrm{nl}},

where
A^{+,\mathrm{nl}}
and
A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}
are decomposable complex 3-forms. The
3
-form
A
is called
nondegenerate
if
A^{+,\mathrm{nl}}\wedge A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}
is everywhere nonvanishing. We parametrize

\displaystyle A^{+,\mathrm{nl}}

\displaystyle=\rho e^{\mu}\omega_{0},

\displaystyle A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}

\displaystyle=\overline{\rho}e^{\overline{\mu}}\overline{\omega}_{0},

where
\rho,\overline{\rho}\in\Omega^{0}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
,
\mu\in\Omega^{-1,1}(M)
,
\overline{\mu}\in\Omega^{1,-1}(M)
and

(129)

\rho e^{\mu}\omega_{0}=\rho\left(\omega_{0}+\mu\omega_{0}+\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}\omega_{0}+\frac{\mu^{3}}{6}\omega_{0}\right).

To write the generating function from the linear to the nonlinear polarization, we use

(130)

G(A^{+,\mathrm{l}},A^{-,\mathrm{nl}})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}A^{+,\mathrm{l}}A^{-,\mathrm{l}}-A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}A^{+,\mathrm{nl}}.

Lemma B.1
.

In the variables
A^{3,0},A^{2,1},\overline{\rho},\overline{\mu}
, the generating function is given by

(131)

G(A^{3,0},A^{2,1},\overline{\rho},\overline{\mu})=\\
=\int_{M}\overline{\rho}(A^{3,0}\overline{\omega}_{0}+A^{2,1}\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0})+\overline{\rho}^{2}\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}-\frac{\left\langle\left((A^{2,1}-\frac{1}{2}\overline{\rho}\,\overline{\mu}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0})^{\vee}\right)^{3}\right\rangle}{(A^{3,0})^{\vee}-\overline{\rho}\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle}\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}.

Proof.

This is a tedious but straightforward computation. One way to do it is to express
G
in terms of
\rho,\mu,\overline{\rho},\overline{\mu}
first. To this end, notice that the decomposition in (
129
) is a decomposition into forms of definite Hodge type. Thus, we can write

(132)

\displaystyle A^{3,0}

\displaystyle={\rho}\,{\omega}_{0}+\overline{\rho}\frac{\overline{\mu}^{3}}{6}\overline{\omega}_{0}=\left(\rho+\overline{\rho}\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle\right)\omega_{0},

(133)

\displaystyle A^{2,1}

\displaystyle={\rho}\,{\mu}\,{\omega}_{0}+\overline{\rho}\frac{\overline{\mu}^{2}}{2}\overline{\omega}_{0},

and similarly for
A^{0,3}
and
A^{1,2}
.
We thus obtain

(134)

\displaystyle A^{3,0}A^{0,3}

\displaystyle=\rho\overline{\rho}(1+\langle\mu^{3}\rangle\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle)\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}+\left(\rho^{2}\langle\mu^{3}\rangle\ +\overline{\rho}^{2}\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle\right)\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0},

(135)

\displaystyle A^{2,1}A^{1,2}

\displaystyle=\rho\overline{\rho}\left(\mu\omega_{0}\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0}+\frac{1}{4}\overline{\mu}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0}\mu^{2}\omega_{0}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\rho^{2}\mu\omega_{0}{\mu}^{2}\omega_{0}-\overline{\rho}^{2}\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0}\overline{\mu}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0}\right).

On the other hand, we have

(136)

A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}A^{+,\mathrm{nl}}=\rho\overline{\rho}\left(\overline{\omega}_{0}\omega_{0}+\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0}\mu\omega_{0}+\frac{1}{4}\overline{\mu}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0}\mu^{2}\omega_{0}+\langle{\overline{\mu}^{3}}\rangle\langle\mu^{3}\rangle\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}\right).

Summing (
134
) and (
135
) and subtracting (
136
), the last two terms in (
136
) cancel and we obtain

(137)

A^{+,\mathrm{l}}A^{-,\mathrm{l}}-A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}A^{+,\mathrm{nl}}=2\rho\overline{\rho}\left(\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}-\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0}\mu\omega_{0}\right)\\
+\rho^{2}\left(\langle\mu^{3}\rangle\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\mu\omega_{0}\mu^{2}\omega_{0}\right)+\overline{\rho}^{2}\left(\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}-\frac{1}{2}\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0}\overline{\mu}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0}\right).

Recall that
\mu\omega_{0}\mu^{2}\omega_{0}=(\mu^{3}\omega_{0})\omega_{0}=6\langle\mu^{3}\rangle\overline{\omega}_{0}\omega_{0}
, hence we can simplify this expression to

A^{+,\mathrm{l}}A^{-,\mathrm{l}}-A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}A^{+,\mathrm{nl}}=2\rho\overline{\rho}\left(\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}-\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0}\mu\omega_{0}\right)-2\left(\rho^{2}\langle\mu^{3}\rangle+\overline{\rho}^{2}\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle\right)\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}.

From equations (
132
), (
133
) we get

\displaystyle\rho\omega

\displaystyle=A^{3,0}-\overline{\rho}\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle\omega_{0},

\displaystyle\rho\mu\omega_{0}

\displaystyle=A^{2,1}-\frac{1}{2}\overline{\rho}\,\overline{\mu}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0},

which we use to rewrite the first term as

2\rho\overline{\rho}\left(\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}-\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0}\mu\omega_{0}\right)=2\overline{\rho}A^{3,0}\overline{\omega}_{0}-2\overline{\rho}^{2}\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle\omega_{0}+2\overline{\rho}A^{2,1}\mu\omega_{0}+6\overline{\rho}^{2}\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}.

In total, (
130
) evaluates to

(138)

G=\int_{M}\overline{\rho}A^{3,0}\overline{\omega}_{0}+\overline{\rho}A^{2,1}\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0}+\overline{\rho}^{2}\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}-\frac{\langle(\rho\mu)^{3}\rangle}{\rho}\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}.

Formula (
131
) may now be obtained by
solving equations (
132
),(
133
) for
\rho
and
\rho\mu
, which gives

(139)

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=\frac{A^{3,0}-\frac{1}{6}\overline{\rho}\,\overline{\mu}^{3}\overline{\omega}_{0}}{\omega_{0}}=(A^{3,0})^{\vee}-\overline{\rho}\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle,

(140)

\displaystyle\rho\mu

\displaystyle=\frac{A^{2,1}-\frac{1}{2}\overline{\rho}\,\overline{\mu}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0}}{\omega_{0}}=\left(A^{2,1}-\frac{1}{2}\overline{\rho}\,\overline{\mu}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0}\right)^{\vee}.

Plugging (
139
),(
140
) into (
138
) we obtain (
131
).
∎

The defining property of
G
is the following.

Lemma B.2
.

We have
\delta G=\theta^{\mathrm{l}}-\theta^{\mathrm{nl}}
where

\theta^{\mathrm{l}}=A^{-,\mathrm{l}}\delta A^{+,\mathrm{l}}
and
\theta^{\mathrm{nl}}=A^{+,\mathrm{nl}}\delta A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}
.

Proof.

This follows from Equation (
130
).
But one can also check it through direct computation: we have

\frac{\delta G}{\delta A^{3,0}}=\overline{\rho}\,\overline{\omega}_{0}+\frac{\left\langle\left(\left(A^{2,1}-\frac{1}{2}\overline{\rho}\,\overline{\mu}^{2}\overline{\omega}\right)^{\vee}\right)^{3}\right\rangle}{(A^{3,0})^{\vee}-\overline{\rho}\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle^{2}}(\omega_{0})^{-1}\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}=\overline{\rho}\,\overline{\omega}_{0}+\frac{\langle(\rho\mu)^{3}\rangle}{\rho^{2}}\overline{\omega}_{0}=A^{3,0}.

Notice that we have

\frac{\delta}{\delta\mu}\langle\mu^{3}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}(\mu^{2}\omega)(\overline{\omega})^{-1}.

It follows that

\frac{\delta}{\delta A^{2,1}}\langle((A^{2,1})^{\vee})^{3}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}(((A^{2,1})^{\vee})^{2}\omega_{0})\overline{\omega}_{0}^{-1}\omega_{0}^{-1}=-\frac{\frac{1}{2}(((A^{2,1})^{\vee})^{2}\omega_{0})}{\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}^{-1}}

(note the sign)
and therefore

\frac{\delta G}{\delta A^{2,1}}=\overline{\rho}\,\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}+\frac{1}{2}\rho\mu^{2}\omega_{0}=A^{1,2}.

This proves that
\delta G/\delta A^{+,\mathrm{l}}=A^{-,\mathrm{l}}
.
Computing
\delta G/\delta\overline{\rho}
gives

\displaystyle\frac{\delta G}{\delta\bar{\rho}}\delta\overline{\rho}

\displaystyle=\delta\overline{\rho}\left(A^{3,0}\overline{\omega}_{0}+A^{2,1}\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0}+2\overline{\rho}\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\rho\mu^{2}\omega_{0}(\frac{1}{2}\overline{\mu}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0})+\rho\langle\mu^{3}\rangle\omega_{0}\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle\overline{\omega}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=-\rho e^{\mu}\omega_{0}e^{\overline{\mu}}\overline{\omega}_{0}\delta\overline{\rho}.

Finally, computing
\delta G/\delta\overline{\mu}
gives

\displaystyle\frac{\delta G}{\delta\overline{\mu}}\delta\overline{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\overline{\rho}A^{2,1}(\delta\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0})+\frac{1}{2}\overline{\rho}^{2}(\overline{\mu}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0})(\delta\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0})+\frac{1}{2}\rho\mu^{2}\omega_{0}(\overline{\rho}\,\overline{\mu}\,\delta\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0})+\rho\langle\mu^{3}\rangle(\delta\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0})\frac{1}{2}\overline{\rho}\,\overline{\mu}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0}

\displaystyle=-\overline{\rho}\rho\,\mu\,\omega_{0}(\delta\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0})+\frac{1}{2}\rho\mu^{2}\omega_{0}(\overline{\rho}\,\overline{\mu}\,\delta\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0})+\rho\langle\mu^{3}\rangle(\delta\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0})\frac{1}{2}\overline{\rho}\,\overline{\mu}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0}

\displaystyle=-\rho\overline{\rho}(e^{\mu}\omega_{0})\delta\overline{\mu}(\overline{\omega}_{0}+\overline{\mu}\omega_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\overline{\mu}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0}).

Using

\delta A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}=\delta\overline{\rho}e^{\overline{\mu}}\overline{\omega}_{0}=e^{\overline{\mu}}\overline{\omega}_{0}\delta\overline{\rho}+\overline{\rho}\delta\overline{\mu}(\overline{\omega}_{0}+\overline{\mu}\omega_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\overline{\mu}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0}),

we obtain

\delta G=A^{+,\mathrm{l}}\delta A^{-,\mathrm{l}}-A^{+,\mathrm{nl}}\delta A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}.

∎

B.3.
Deformations of complex structures

Let
M
be a compact Calabi–Yau manifold supplied with a reference holomorphic 3-form
\omega_{0}
. A deformation of the complex structure is equivalent to a deformation of the
\bar{\partial}
operator
\bar{\partial}\to\bar{\partial}_{\bar{A}}=\bar{\partial}+\bar{A}
, where
\bar{A}\in\Omega^{-1,1}(M)=\Gamma(T^{1,0}M\otimes(T^{*})^{0,1}M)
. The integrability condition
\bar{\partial}_{A}^{2}=0
is equivalent to (
[
31
]
)

(141)

\bar{\partial}\bar{A}+\frac{1}{2}[\bar{A},\bar{A}]=0.

The moduli space of complex structures is thus given by solutions of (
141
) modulo gauge transformations

(142)

\delta\bar{A}=\bar{\partial}\varepsilon+[\bar{A},\varepsilon],

with
\varepsilon\in\Omega^{-1,0}(M)
. The tangent space to the moduli space of complex structures is given by the linearization of (
141
), i.e., it is the quotient of
\{\alpha\colon\bar{\partial}\bar{\alpha}=0\}
by linearized gauge transformations
\delta\alpha=\bar{\partial}\varepsilon
.

After Tian (
[
40
]
), this problem can be reformulated using
\bar{A}^{\vee}
as follows. Imposing the constraint
\partial\bar{A}^{\vee}=0
and using (
128
), we can rewrite (
141
) as

(143)

\bar{\partial}\bar{A}^{\vee}+\partial(\bar{A}^{\vee}\circ\bar{A}^{\vee})=0.

B.4.
Kodaira–Spencer action

The Kodaira–Spencer action functional as introduced in
[
9
]
is

(144)

S_{KS}[\bar{A}^{\vee}]=\int_{M}\frac{1}{2}\bar{A}^{\vee}\partial^{-1}\bar{\partial}\bar{A}^{\vee}+\frac{1}{6}\langle\bar{A}^{\vee},\bar{A}^{\vee},\bar{A}^{\vee}\rangle.

Here the first term is well-defined due to
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma. The equation of motion of (
144
) is (
143
). One can resolve the nonlocality by writing
\bar{A}^{\vee}=x+\partial b
, where
x
is a
\partial
-harmonic
(2,1)
-form. The action functional then becomes

(145)

S_{KS}(x;b)=\int_{M}\frac{1}{2}\partial b\bar{\partial}b+\frac{1}{6}\langle(x+\partial b),(x+\partial b),(x+\partial b)\rangle.

This action functional has the following remarkable property.
From eq. (
143
), it follows that any harmonic
(2,1)
-form
x=\bar{A}^{\vee}_{1}
can be interpreted as a first order deformation of the complex structure. The tree level diagrams of (
145
) then generate forms
\bar{A}^{\vee}_{n}
with the property that
\bar{A}^{\vee}=\sum\varepsilon^{n}\bar{A}^{\vee}_{n}
is a solution of the Kodaira–Spencer equation (
143
). We refer to
[
9
, Section 5.2]
for details.
